Sticky inflation and resilient growth risk would likely limit space for easing

● RBA and BoC remain hawkish relative to their DM counterparts

China CPI shows deeper slide into deflation

• Next week: Soft US CPI and RS; European labor market reports

## It’s not how you fall that matters; it’s how you land

Timing the start of central bank easing cycles has been a significant market focus in recent months. We have pushed back against a rising tide of market optimism for an early start and recent central bank rhetoric, along with evidence of US and global growth resilience, is now aligning expectations with our view that easing is more likely to begin around midyear. Despite this shift, there remains a firm consensus that substantial easing lies ahead. Both our economists and market pricing incorporate a roughly 200bp decline in policy rates for the Fed, ECB, and BoE by the end of next year (Figure 1). While market pricing incorporates some risk of aggressive easing in the event of a recession, the easing in broader financial conditions of late points to a close linkage between rising risk appetite and optimism that substantial central bank easing will lay the groundwork for a sustained expansion.

From a top-down perspective, we have recognized the improved prospects for a soft-landing and now see this outcome as a roughly equal in likelihood to a “boil the frog<sup>”</sup> slide toward recession. Recent developments showing solid private sector income gains and still healthy balance sheets, combined with easing financial conditions, are promoting resilience. Nevertheless, we remain skeptical of a soft-landing as these same developments also carry risks for more limited central bank easing.

Despite tight policy, global growth has proceeded at an above-trend pace in recent quarters, with our economists<sup>’</sup> forecast for GDP gains moving steadily higher in recent months (Figure 2). Sustained solid growth creates two pressure points. First, it suggests that the reverberation of the pandemic shock has pushed neutral policy rates higher. Second, it limits the alignment of demand and supply that central

banks anticipate will relieve tight labor markets. Strong US growth is primarily challenging the Fed<sup>’</sup>s views around its neutral rate in an environment in which robust labor force gains have matched employment increases. Although growth has been stagnant in Western Europe, continued labor market tightening signals weak supply-side performance and keeps wage inflation a threat to central bank inflation targets. Inflation took a significant step lower in 2H23, raising optimism that fading supply shocks will do much of the work in bringing inflation back to target. But our economists are forecasting a stall in the path back to inflation targets. Last quarter<sup>’</sup>s slide into goods price deflation is expected to reverse while service price inflation remains elevated. On balance, our CPI forecasts calls for global core inflation to remain at about a 3%ar in 1H24. A failure to deliver the further leg down in inflation later this year that is incorporated in our forecasts would significantly limit the space for central bank easing over the coming two years.

A faster and more substantial disinflation has been recorded in the core PCE, which rose at 2% during 2H23 to match the Fed<sup>’</sup>s target rate. Doubts around the sustainability of a sharp fall in core goods and supercore services have promoted caution. But even with our expectation for a firming in core PCE inflation during 1H24, recent PCE price movements bolster the FOMC median forecast that a near-complete return to targeted inflation can be established by next year, even as growth proceeds at close to a trend pace.

Viewing the evolution of these parameters through the lens of a balanced Taylor rule is useful for evaluating the sensitivity of central bank reaction functions to potential shifts in perceptions of the macroeconomic landscape. Incorporating the FOMC<sup>’</sup>s Survey of Economic Projections (SEP) for 2025, together with an estimated 1% real policy rate—modestly above the SEP long-run rate estimate—yields a 3.3% implied policy rate. This Taylor rule prescription aligns with current FOMC guidance—the SEP projects 3.6% in 4Q25—as well as current market and J.P. Morgan forecasts. However, the picture changes significantly if perceptions shift alongside sustained growth surprises. Outcomes in which the unemployment rate is perceived to remain unchanged at its current level (3.7%) and core PCE inflation is expected to settle modestly higher than current forecasts (2.5%) would likely also prompt a modest upward revision to estimates of near-term neutral. While each of these has a modest impact on prescribed policy rates for next year, the set of correlated shifts presented in Table 1 push the prescribed policy rate back toward 5%. While still consistent with rate cuts this year, this exercise highlights that the recent strength of US growth and employment has increased the risk of a reassessment that can challenge optimism about the magnitude of the easing cycle ahead.

## Hawks dig in at RBA, linger at BoC

While several DM central banks have formally pivoted to more dovish stances and communicated easing will start around midyear, the RBA held its near-term tightening bias this week. We had expected it to shift to a more neutral stance while still pushing back against cuts, so the tone was surprisingly hawkish. With a downgraded staff forecast still indicating above-target inflation until 2025, we continue to expect the RBA to remain on hold this year. Meanwhile, sticky core inflation, slowly moderating (but still elevated) wage growth, and continued labor market resilience also give a hawkish lean to the BoC outlook. Bank rhetoric has suggested the BoC is looking to hold out as long as possible, and we think a June start to its easing cycle is most likely given the data on hand.

## EM easing to pick up speed

While DM central banks have mostly pushed back on early easing expectations, the combination of falling inflation, easier global financial conditions, and elevated real policy rates has allowed several EM central banks to continue ongoing cutting cycles, with an expected acceleration in the pace in some instances. This week Czechia sped up its cutting pace with a 50bp move to 6.25%. Given greater confidence at the CNB that inflation has moved lower (and less concern about currency weakness), we look for 50bp cuts through May, with additional 25bp cuts per meeting to bring the policy rate to 4% at year-end. We also look for Hungary to accelerate its easing pace later this month, while we expect both Chile and Colombia to deliver larger cuts in March. Mexico has lagged the rest of LatAm and held policy steady as anticipated this week, but we look for Banxico<sup>’</sup>s easing cycle to engage next month and potentially pick up steam as it progresses.

By contrast, concern about regional currencies and interestrate differentials against the US have resulted in more delayed easing moves among EM Asian central banks. However, downside surprises to inflation have opened up space for additional easing this year, provided the Fed has started reducing rates by then. This week we penciled in cuts for Thailand and added more cuts in the Philippines—an additional 50bp of easing for both during 2H24. The RBI appears in no hurry to ease after remaining on hold this week and retaining a more cautious tone than we had expected. While liquidity operations are easing the monetary stance in India for now, we look for post-Fed RBI rate cuts during 2H24.

## China: Doctor, doctor, give me the news

China slipped further into deflation last month with its CPI dropping a larger-than-expected 0.8%oya. While an oversupply of pork and easing energy prices account for this deflationary slide, core CPI is up only 0.4%oya in January—an indication of weak domestic demand. Although the recent firming in export and PPI inflation suggests a moderating deflationary impact on global goods prices, weak overall nominal GDP growth (3.7%oya in 4Q23) points to a lack of income generation for both private businesses and household spending (Figure 3). With policymakers seemingly focused on real GDP growth, which expanded a solid 5.2%oya last quarter, the risk is that the imbalances within the economy are not being diagnosed correctly. Material monetary easing beyond our forecast for two minor 10bp cuts (the first coming in March) is unlikely. This owes in part to margin pressure on banks but also to the PBOC<sup>’</sup>s benign view of the inflation outlook: the latest Monetary Policy Report argued that inflation is poised to pick up. Against this backdrop, rising momentum for credit support offers some potential relief. The bounce in TSF for January reported today is largely consistent with the usual surge in lending at the start of the year, but the magnitude was a touch stronger than expected. A notable rise in bank loans is an encouraging sign, however, that demand may be coming back.

## Europe: Watching the consumer

European growth was stagnant through the end of last year, despite further growth in household real incomes. With incomes set to remain supportive, we expect consumers to become less cautious and spark a pickup in 1H24 growth.

In Central Europe retail sales data have been improving as expected, as last quarter saw gains build on a recovery that started in 3Q23. Sales volumes expanded at an average 6%ar, led by a double-digit increase in Poland. In the UK, evidence of green shoots is emerging. Falling mortgage rates and improving sentiment appear to be generating a revival in housing market activity, which has typically been associated with stronger growth. The PMI in the UK stands at 52.5, and is already flagging upside risk to our 1% forecast for 1H24.

Should these dynamics build in the coming months, it could require another nudge higher in our call for growth to stall in 2H24. In the Euro area, green shoots are harder to spot. The composite PMI has clawed its way back to a level suggesting stagnation, but retail sales fell 1.1%m/m in December. The Euro area consumer remains a puzzle as the recovery in real incomes looks solid, credit drags are easing, and consumers inflation perceptions are slowly catching up with the much larger fall in inflation.

## CBRT signals high for longer

In Türkiye, President Erdogan appointed Deputy Governor Fatih Karahan as CBRT governor, after Hafize Gaye Erkan resigned. While sudden leadership changes can sometimes roil markets, we see this move as positive for macroeconomic stabilization. In this week<sup>’</sup>s quarterly Inflation Report press briefing, the newly-appointed governor stated that no further hikes are needed, but pledged to keep the policy rate high for longer given elevated core inflation (70.5%oya in January). The CBRT also kept the door open for further rate hikes should the inflation outlook deteriorate. As a result, we are more confident that the CBRT is likely to keep its policy rate at 45% (or higher) this year, without any premature rate cuts.

## Growth to accelerate in Brazil and Mexico

While 4Q23 growth was weak, we look for it to re-accelerate this year in both Brazil and Mexico. In Brazil, activity indicators have been favorable outside of December<sup>’</sup>s retail sales; the January PMIs rebounded to levels not seen in at least a year and a half. This backdrop appears consistent with BCB<sup>’</sup>s guidance for continued 50bp rate cuts during 1H24. We still see downside risks to our baseline terminal rate of 9.5% for Brazil, but these have mitigated somewhat. Meanwhile, activity in Mexico hit a pothole in 4Q23 but we still look for GDP to bounce to 3%ar this quarter. A pre-election boost to government spending and a revival in gross fixed investment should be the catalyst for this pickup.

## Nowcast of global growth: Another PMI-induced tick up

The J.P. Morgan forecast for 1Q24 global real GDP growth remained unchanged this week at 2.4%ar (Figure 1). In the latest sign of solid global growth momentum, the January allindustry PMI rose for a third consecutive month to 51.8, a level consistent with 2.8%ar global growth. Broad-based improvements across sectors and economies provided some hope that recent lackluster data from the manufacturing sector and Euro area could turn a corner in coming months. For now, however, the sectoral and regional divides seen over much of the last year look likely to persist at least in the near term. Moreover, recent data on retail sales and capex point to some softening in final goods demand growth. The Euro area consumer ended 2023 on a downbeat note despite ongoing real income gains, and a moderation in our capex nowcaster makes a pickup in final demand look less assured. Nevertheless, we continue to expect solid fundamentals to ensure modest growth in both the Euro area and global goods sector in the coming quarters.

Figure 1: J.P. Morgan global GDP % change saar; forecast by date made
![](images/671d77675fe78c5d21e556a4d00914b2965c2077c0ed65c1f99ee0d02a44ee8a.jpg)

An upside surprise in the global service PMI helped push the top-down global nowcaster up a further 0.1%-pt this week to 1.8%ar, suggesting 0.6%-pt of downside risk to our forecast (Figure 2). The bottom-up nowcaster meanwhile moved sideways, continuing to flag 0.3%-pt of upside risk to our forecast for the same country set. The nowcaster continues to see upside risks to growth in China (+1.8%-pts), but downside risk in the US (-0.6%-pt) and Euro area (-0.6%-pt).

This week<sup>’</sup>s data news was headlined by the release of the January global all-industry PMI. At 51.8, the output PMI points to 2.8%ar growth in global GDP, implying some upside risk to our 2.4%ar forecast. This level of the PMI is an 0.8pt improvement from December, supported by a 0.9pt and 0.7pt gain in the manufacturing and service output PMIs, respectively. Other recent survey data have been similarly encouraging. Global business confidence continued its recent recovery with a 0.3pt gain in January (albeit from a low level), driven by a 1.9pt jump in the future output PMI. Consumer confidence has also made headway in recent months; strength in the US last month lifted global (ex China) consumer confidence to its highest level in two years.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bond yields ground higher over the week amid pushback from central bank speakers over near-term rate cuts and ongoing strength in activity and labor market data in the US. Markets are now pricing in around 110bp of Fed cuts by end-2024 vs. our economists<sup>’</sup> expectations of 125bp; as the near term economic data flow should be more supportive of lower yields, and with a reduced long base, we turn bullish on duration and add longs in 5Y USTs. We also keep 5s/30s steepeners as a medium-term trade on eventual cuts and some repricing of term premia.

In the Euro area, rates have sold off in sympathy with global rates and some pushback from ECB board members against early cuts. We continue to see soft domestic fundamentals to eventually drive yields lower and stay long 5Y Germany, but keep hedges as markets are pushing a first cut further back. In the UK, we remove a bearish bias on May MPC OIS. We hold Mar24/Sep24 SONIA futures curve flatteners.

In EM, the overall environment remains supportive given resilient growth, falling inflation and cautious rate cuts, provided our baseline of Fed cuts starting from mid-year materializes. We stay OW local bond duration, with OWs in Latam (Brazil, Colombia, Peru and Uruguay) and EMEA EM (Czechia, Poland, South Africa), and MW EM Asia, partially offset by a short duration overlay and UWs in Romania (EMOS, Feb 9<sup>th</sup>).

## Credit

US HG bond spreads consolidate with strong equities, active supply. The equity market strength (US equity markets reached new records) didn<sup>’</sup>t translate into record-tight US HG bond spreads, which is a trend we expect to continue. This is not only because equities are trending markets and HG bonds are range bound but also because we expect HG companies to continue to issue bonds actively. January was a record supply month for the HG, HY, and loan markets at \$361Bn combined. February issuance may significantly exceed the recent February \$107bn average, as was the case in January where supply also was well above the historical trend. So far the market has absorbed this supply easily with spreads currently at 109bp, just 4bp off of the post-GFC tight. We do believe current spreads are too tight, though we do not expect a strong sell-off near term. Another trend that has been evident in recent weeks is outperformance of Financials vs Non-Financials. This we believe will continue, but the narrowing may come from weakness in Non-Financials given tight spreads and given that most February supply is likely to be in Non-Financials (Credit Market Outlook & Strategy, Feb 9<sup>th</sup>).

Figure 1: Year-to-date returns %
![](images/750c31bbd9bb779eca76cab8296b3f65471ce3f6802a9a0c343b4899204fab32.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan Global Economics

Technicals are in control in Euro credit, which continued to grind tighter this week as spreads absorbed part of the move higher in sovereign yields. Altogether, it feels like the technicals remain firmly in command of the market, with another €600m of inflows into euro investment grade funds this week, taking the year-to-date total to just under €7bn, or almost half the volume from the entirety of last year. In our view, there is not enough paper on offer to absorb demand. While supply volumes ticked up this week as issuers began to exit their quarterly blackout periods, average new issue premiums are still holding near - or in some cases just below - zero. We think that primary volumes will underwhelm over the coming months, with issuers having only limited new funding needs and a lot of refinancing front-loaded into the first few weeks of the year (European Credit Weekly, Feb 9<sup>th</sup>).

## Currencies

New Year, new risks. The motivation for our bullish USD forecasts (EUR/USD 1.03-1.05 in 1H; USD/CNY 7.25 in 1Q) is persistent US growth and yield exceptionalism. This outlook is unchanged. The dollar<sup>’</sup>s yield advantage will stay intact even with Fed rate cuts given how synchronized the easing cycle will be. On growth, US exceptionalism isn<sup>’</sup>t dead yet. Even though China/ Eurozone growth momentum has stabilized, the US is still getting upgraded. Relative US productivity is also improving which is USD-supportive. Tariffs are garnering more media attention and increasing risk premia in FX. Prospects of tariffs would make the durability of the basing China/ EU growth questionable. Expectations of the

most synchronized central bank rate cuts in recent history keeps RV based on carry-to-value rotation in play, but this needs lower rates to materialize.

Forecast revisions: G10: GBP is marked higher on BoE pushback; EUR/GBP 2Q 0.87 (0.88); GBP/USD 2Q 1.21 (1.19). Rest unchanged; EUR/USD 1.03-1.05 in 1H. USD/ JPY 4Q 144. EM: ZAR upgraded in 2H on a hawkish SARB and favorable fiscal and growth prospects. USD/ZAR 4Q 18.30 (18.50). USD/CNY 1Q 7.25. USD/MXN 2Q 17.25. USD/BRL 2Q 5.00 (all unchanged). (KCV, Feb 9<sup>th</sup>)

## Commodities

Our Brent outlook continues to project a tightening market with prices rising by another \$10 by May. Our constructive price outlook assumes zero geopolitical premium and a view that Saudi Arabia and Russia will bring a combined 400 kbd of their voluntary cuts back into the market starting from April. The way to higher prices is through lower inventories. If our supply-demand forecast holds, the oil market will be in a marginal 300 kbd surplus this year, comprising a relatively benign 400 kbd stock build in the first quarter before inventories start to draw by mid-year. This can easily shift to a balanced market if OPEC+ maintains supply restraint and extends voluntary production cuts through yearend. In this scenario, inventories will begin shrinking as soon as April (Oil Markets Weekly, Feb 8<sup>th</sup>).

Seasonal metals inventory builds are normal for now and stocks remain low. While Chinese equity markets rebounded sharply this week on expectations of equity-market focused stimulus, base metals prices continued to slump into the Lunar New Year (LNY) holiday. Despite pessimism on current Chinese metals demand conditions, stock builds in the run up to the holiday have been average (copper) or modestly lower than average (aluminum and zinc) so far, even with higher yoy production last month. While the bulk of seasonal inventory builds still remain ahead of us and China will now go quiet for the next few weeks, we think base metals prices are approaching levels that look cheap given supply risks emerging across the complex and data suggesting an improving, rather than cooling global economy (Metals Weekly— China Base Metals Inventory Update, Feb 8<sup>th</sup>).
