The ISM services survey's headline composite increased from 50.5 in December to 53.4 in January, beating expectations. This solid improvement came after a drop reported for December, and it is not clear that this recent volatility is reflective of a meaningful change in economic conditions across these months. This volatility has been particularly severe in the survey<sup>’</sup>s employment index, which fell 6.8-pts in December to a very downbeat level of 43.8 before jumping 6.7-pts in January, but the sharp deterioration reported for December does not appear to be evident in many of the other labor market indicators that we track. So for now we think that some of the recent ups and downs in the ISM survey data are just noise and unlikely reflective of more fundamental changes in the economy.

International trade (Feb 7) \$billion, samr

In some of the other details of the ISM survey, the business activity index held at 55.8 between December and January, coming in at a lackluster level for an economic expansion. The supplier deliveries index climbed 2.9-pts to 52.4 in January (not seasonally adjusted). There has been a noticeable move up in the measure since early in 2023, but this increase came off of very low levels, and the recent figures are not particularly unusual by pre-pandemic standards and far below many of the figures reported throughout much of 2020-2022.

<table><tr><td colspan="2"></td><td rowspan="2">Oct</td><td colspan="2">Nov</td><td colspan="2">Dec</td></tr><tr><td>Balance(BOP basis)</td><td>-64.5 -63.9</td><td>-63.2</td><td>-61.9</td><td>61.9</td><td>-62.2</td></tr><tr><td>Services</td><td>25.5</td><td></td><td>26.2</td><td>26.5</td><td>26.6</td><td>26.9</td></tr><tr><td>Merchandise</td><td></td><td>-90.0 -89.4</td><td>-89.4</td><td>-88.4</td><td>88.5</td><td>-89.1</td></tr><tr><td>Exports (%m/m)</td><td>-1.1</td><td></td><td>-1.9</td><td>-1.6</td><td>19</td><td>1.5</td></tr><tr><td>Imports (%m/m)</td><td>0.2</td><td></td><td>-1.9</td><td></td><td>出</td><td>1.3</td></tr></table>

The nominal trade balance widened from -\$61.9bn in November to -\$62.2bn in December. The December deficit was pretty close to expectations, but earlier readings were revised to be narrower than what was previously shown. So while the end point was similar to what we and the consensus had anticipated, it was surprising that the deficit widened to that level (vs. expectations for narrowing in December). On the whole, the trade data for 4Q now look stronger than was anticipated with respect to GDP that quarter, and as a result we are raising our tracking estimate of 4Q real GDP growth from 3.5% to 3.6% saar. The monthly trade figures are choppy, but goods export volumes increased in 4Q while related import volumes were roughly unchanged, and this combination led to some narrowing in the real goods deficit between 3Q and 4Q. For the nominal services trade data, export growth generally has been stronger than import growth recently, and this continued into the December data.

# Focus: Rent inflation is cooling but unlikely in free fall

It is useful to track “alternative<sup>”</sup> rent inflation measures given that shelter inflation in the CPI basket constitutes around 40% of the core inflation measure when we think about rent of primary residences and owners<sup>’</sup> equivalent rent combined. The corresponding weight in the core PCE measure is lower, slightly below 18%. The New Tenant Repeat Rent (NTRR) index is a relatively new addition to these alternative measures and has attracted attention as it seems to be leading the turning points in the CPI shelter inflation (Figure 1). The NTRR fell sharply in 4Q, diverging a lot from some other commonly discussed measures, such as the Zillow Observed Rent Index (ZORI). We argue that one needs to discount the last data point for the NTRR in real time given the possibility for revisions (as highlighted by the BLS overview here) and its divergence from some other related measures. While revisions could boost the recent NTRR data over time, we still think that rent inflation will keep moderating in the official CPI measures in the near feature.

Figure 1: Alternative rent inflation measures
![](images/26acbcb4d4f7f62e656e1e60cd745d6b7d3efd0931d35ad1c6fc77931516053c.jpg)

Recent work by researchers from the BLS and the Fed led to this new research series by the BLS that could provide early signals about the direction of the CPI shelter inflation. This new NTRR index is now being updated quarterly and released by the BLS as a research index series. The main advantage of this new index stems from its ability to control for the composition of the housing units by only focusing on those units in the BLS housing survey that appear in repeated transactions. A unit with a new tenant and a new rental contract helps control for difficult to measure unobserved characteristics across units. The measure comes from the same universe which the CPI data covers, and as such, is nationally representative. It is also clear that the NTRR index leads CPI shelter inflation with a few quarters around cyclical turning points (Figure 1). The longer time series relative to ZORI confirms this regularity for the GFC episode as well.

But since the NTRR tracks repeat transactions, the sample size is very small relative to the universe of all housing units tracked by the CPI shelter inflation. More importantly, as units vacate and new tenants move in, new observations for those units appear in the calculation of the index, which will retrospectively change the most recent data points. As the research paper and the BLS information page describe, this is by construction and is due to the focus on repeated transactions. Since fewer number of new rental contracts are signed in the first and the last quarters of the year, these data points are more susceptible to revisions too. The index is relatively new so we lack a long history to analyze the first prints systematically, but even the limited evidence confirms this point. For instance, the first reading for 231Q reported in the aforementioned paper was -0.10%oya and it currently stands at 1.1%oya in the official updated data.

We suspect that if the very large drop in the NTRR inflation in 4Q was truly reflecting the fundamentals in the rental market, we would have seen a large increase in related rental vacancies. But this increase in vacancies did not occur. While vacancies are not the only determining factor of rental inflation, the 4Q observation for the NTRR stands out as a clear outlier based on the historic relationship to vacancies (Figure 2). The 4Q print for the ZORI—which was not nearly as weak—is more in line with the historic norms associated with rental vacancies (Figure 3).

Figure 2: Rental vacancies versus NTRR (1Q16-4Q23)
![](images/26d0b9d8f8575081539cb50f3af0958f0ac53369a39147882ac317c6852aa495.jpg)
Source: Census Bureau, BLS, J.P. Morgan

Figure 3: Rental vacancies versus ZORI (1Q16-4Q23)
![](images/26875745c2d97a9a30b1ee1079684ce8153440ac99a0f57fd47a781cd144606f.jpg)
Source: Census Bureau, Zillow, J.P. Morgan

## Euro Area

• Euro area composite PMI still close to stagnation level, but key details are improving

Retail sales are struggling to lift, but real incomes still point to higher spending

• German industry remains weak, but some aspects remain puzzling

This week has been lighter on data and has not moved the debate about the economic and monetary policy outlook on much further. Nevertheless, it confirmed a further modest improvement in the PMI, with details hinting at the possibility of further improvement. Hard data remain more challenging, with retail sales and German IP both disappointing. But, even here, there are signs that the data can turn higher in the coming months. In any case, none of this is hurting the labor market much, which is giving the ECB time to watch the price and wage data before deciding on when to cut rates.

## PMI still weak with rays of light

This week<sup>’</sup>s final composite PMI did not result in any meaningful revisions. This left the composite output index up 0.3pt in January and consistent with only marginal GDP growth. To validate our 1Q24 GDP forecast of 0.5%q/q saar, the PMI still needs to increase by 1pt. However, some details remain encouraging and suggest that the PMI could improve further in the near-term.

First, the future output index has improved significantly and was never as depressed as the current output index (Figure 1). Second, the composite new orders index has improved by over 3pts recently, with almost half of that coming in January. Third, an improvement in manufacturing has somewhat closed the growth gap to services, even though it still points to contracting IP. Fourth, country details suggest that parts of the region have improved, most clearly the periphery and in a more choppy way Germany (Figure 2). Finally, the labor market details remain solid, pointing to ongoing job creation.

Figure 1: Euro area composite PMI
![](images/f3512deacc4b6097f4f710ae208223d9710768cc9be0ef25cb7c0f089fab1299.jpg)

Figure 2: Euro area composite PMI
![](images/e9487ebb7b9df5647412885fa3d647c22b54f026ff32b229c1703999b5b9b9be.jpg)
Source: Markit, J.P. Morgan

## Still mixed indications on the consumer

A key part of our forecast of a modest improvement in Euro area GDP growth is that consumer spending responds to a solid turn higher in real incomes. This week<sup>’</sup>s retail sales report however showed a decline of 1.1%m/m in December, which more than reversed recent increases and left retail sales down 0.4%q/q saar in 4Q23 (Figure 3). At the same time, the linkage between retail sales and total consumer spending has been loose since the start of the pandemic.

Figure 3: Euro area consumer spending
![](images/98dd5556ae8d585a619a64d5d8040a429f67d4c7650c710bb71bcb9e01cbbf0f.jpg)

The details also highlight a complex picture. At the country level, the downward drift reflects very heterogeneous developments (Figure 4). Spain has seen a solid upward trajectory over the past two years, France is broadly stable at a high level and Germany and Italy have been moving lower. Equally, the weakness at the Euro area level still owes a lot to lower spending on food, rather than on other goods (Figure 5). Finally, we note that there is uncertainty about the December prints. In Germany, these have tended to be very unreliable due to late responses over the holiday period, the shifting pattern of seasonal discounts and the increased popularity of gift vouchers. The latter shifts some spending from December to January. As a result, it is too early to draw very strong conclusions about December itself.

Figure 4: Euro area retail sales
![](images/22f54fa1d65e7e9c1951454ccde7c94ddd028ab0b03db4a597c37f15f9df98af.jpg)

Figure 5: Euro area retail sales 1Q19=100, volumes
![](images/69321bc86323c4e60c790b38f4c0721eb810cd778cdd0a856ff38a80b6e9dbb2.jpg)

One factor that may still be relevant is that consumers are not yet perceiving a large decline in inflation. The latest ECB Consumer Expectations Survey showed inflation perceptions falling, but remaining much higher than actual headline inflation (Figure 6). Nevertheless, the push from real incomes continues to look strong, reflecting high nominal wage growth, ongoing job creation and a persistent slowing in headline inflation. Even with relatively cautious assumptions, real incomes should growth by at least a 1%ar this year.

Figure 6: Euro area consumer expectations survey
![](images/b0227a4b5dc04a895a1147d584d5d278ec1f6405dee90e36408d25cde271ea4d.jpg)

## Germany holding back Euro area IP

Only a few countries have published IP data for December so far. These were mixed, but there are more striking divergences in terms of 4Q23 as a whole. In particular, Germany has reported a decline of 8.4%q/q saar, while France was flat, Italy was up 1.1%m/m, Spain was up 1.5%q/q saar and Portugal was up 2.9%q/q saar. The Netherlands also looks much firmer on manufacturing.

As has been the case for a long time now, the German data contain some internal inconsistencies. We have highlighted many times the high order backlogs, which cast some doubt on the narrative that German industry has weakened as a result of structural headwinds (Figure 7). There is also an ongoing gap between factory sales and manufacturing output. Similarly, IP looks weaker than the manufacturing value added in the GDP data (Figure 8). The latter tend to get revised more comprehensively over time and therefore suggest that the sector is performing better in level terms than suggested by the IP data. At the same time, the PMI still suggests that German IP is contracting on a trend basis. So far, only the pace of contraction has eased.

Figure 7: German industrial indicators
![](images/472932988c1f4d1c1a69ac4b9af369a27a6f29b5b02d0b3dab2e8c4e13f57f95.jpg)

Figure 8: German manufacturing output
![](images/d05d30e198cf60e295ab6ec88b3854ddb67883181c97232e0bc5e8e89dbe5c8c.jpg)

The flash report showed a slight increase in Euro area GDP of 0.1%q/q saar. National data remain consistent with this for 4Q23, but point to some modest upward revisions to prior quarters. For example, 3Q23 could get revised up by 0.2%-pt to -0.3%q/q saar. On an ex-Ireland basis, Euro area GDP likely averaged 0.4%ar over the past year, with 4Q23 at 0.3%q/q saar. This is because the distorted Irish data have been a net drag over the past year.

<table><tr><td></td><td>Industrial production</td><td></td><td>Oct</td><td>Nov</td><td>Dec</td></tr><tr><td>Wed</td><td>Euro area</td><td>Sep</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Feb 14</td><td>Ind production (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-0.8</td><td>-0.7</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.3</td></tr><tr><td>11:00am</td><td>%oya, sa</td><td>-6.3</td><td>-5.7</td><td>-6.4</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Manuf prod (%m/m, sa)</td><td>-0.5</td><td>-0.7</td><td>-0.2</td><td>0.2</td></tr></table>

Country releases so far point to IP declines in Germany (-1.2%m/m) and Spain (-0.3%), while IP was up 1.1% in France and Italy (and jumped 6.8%m/m in the Netherlands). This hints at a 0.3%m/m gain in Euro area IP ex. construction in December. But the volatile Irish number is unknown so far and could create some surprise. The details will be important to monitor. Indications of a rebound in the energy-intensive sector is key for the manufacturing outlook.

## Japan

• Scheduled earnings continue to trend up through noise

• December consumption data round off a weak quarter

• Next week: 4Q GDP report confirms activity airpocket through year-end

Total cash earnings rose a disappointing 1.0%oya in December, reflecting a softer-than-expected contribution from special (i.e., year-end bonus) payments. These rose only 0.5%oya, against our expectation for a more robust rise. Given strong corporate profit momentum reported in the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s 4Q Tankan survey that should have supported winter bonus payments, we suspect the data are not providing a complete picture of total wage growth (Figures 1 and 2). This in turn likely reflects persistent sampling issues that can create volatility in the monthly wage statistics.

Figure 1: Japan Total cash earnings
![](images/6152c457d38510bd103a8192c15ec36d8afeb2a5a669e5721c6c265ed5b13430.jpg)

Figure 2: Composition of total cash earnings JPY thousands, both scales, sa
![](images/a0becaf62ac145f0338ff270614af0bea5bee23eee329921047ac2f286f377f4.jpg)

There are two measures that we think provide a better indication of underlying wage growth, and that form inputs into the BoJ<sup>’</sup>s tracking of wage momentum. Both of these remained robust through year-end. First, scheduled earnings, which are not distorted by volatile special payments, rose 1.6%oya from 1.0% in November. This marks the strongest pace of growth in more than two decades bar May 2023, when scheduled earnings were only a touch stronger at 1.7%. Second, removing sampling issues, constant-base wage data look even more robust. Scheduled earnings on this metric were up 2.2%oya, their fourth consecutive month of above-2% growth.

The volatile monthly wage data can be misleading in gauging the underlying trend in economy-wide wage growth. But December data suggest momentum remains robust. Part-time scheduled earnings are settling around a 4%oya clip, implying wages for part-timers are keeping up with inflation, albeit modestly (Figure 3). Real wage growth remains negative for full-timers, but we suspect this trend will improve amid robust outcomes from the upcoming Spring wage negotiations, with the first official round slated for mid-March.

Figure 3: Japan Scheduled earnings composition %oya, both scales
![](images/eeb42cd661ef810180cb528c9496646be22cae26378a925a0292e62c5281a2d2.jpg)

## Consumption falls in December

The BoJ<sup>’</sup>s Consumption Activity Index (CAI, travel balance adjusted) plunged 2.0%m/m, sa, in December, leaving the 4Q average down 4.6%q/q, saar, compared to 3Q. In December, goods consumption, both for durables and nondurables, was weak, falling 3.6%m/m, sa. Meanwhile, services consumption held almost steady, with a 0.1% rise, but declines in the earlier months left the 4Q average 1.4%q/q, saar below 3Q. While some of the weakness was attributable to the warm winter, the recent consumption indicators lack a sense of strength, in part reflecting average wage growth that has yet to catch up with inflation (Figure 4).

Figure 4: BoJ Consumption activity index
![](images/a0d349bb61ab6ee0d8e768b2cf538b2e51a9c8da64fde2743c91bc7478181719.jpg)

The soft consumption tracking data aligns with weak retail sales data released last week, and challenges Governor Ueda<sup>’</sup>s

January post-meeting press conference remarks where he said “we have received various side evidence that consumption during the year-end and New Year holidays is somewhat strong.<sup>”</sup> While we think recently volatile monthly CAI are not necessarily good at tracking GDP-based consumption, the result poses downside risk to our 4Q GDP-based consumption forecasts of -0.2%q/q, saar (Figure 5). While consumer sentiment is trending up, reflecting a gradual moderation in inflation and rising wage prospects, we remain skeptical that the BoJ can start raising the policy rate in coming months if a consumption air pocket is hitting the economy.

Employers’ survey – preliminary (Feb 6)
Figure 5: Real consumption %q/q, saar
![](images/4e368a268a4c3c3fdb652835cec6f4e737a37e1eb035b23597c24c45ba7df57d.jpg)

## Canada

• Employment surprised to the upside in January

• Wage growth moderated last month but remained heated

Governor Macklem is resolute: monetary policy is not the right tool to address housing affordability

Top line labor market news reported this week was solid in January. Employment increased by 37,000 in the month, the best performance in four months. Growth beat expectations and even our more optimistic forecast. The unemployment rate fell a tick to 5.7% as we expected— the first decline in the unemployment rate since December 2022. Wage growth (AHE) eased moderately in January but remained elevated at 5.3%oya following an increase of 5.7%oya in December 2023 (permanent workers). Growth in AHE has been at or above 5.0%oya for 11 out of the last 12 months (Figure 1). Wage growth remains sharply above pre-pandemic trends. This aspect of the labor market has garnered the most attention from the Bank of Canada and it<sup>’</sup>s likely that sustained heated wage growth has led to very tempered market expectations of a BoC cut in 1H24. We maintain our forecast that the Bank will cut in 2Q24. Bank rhetoric suggests that the Bank is looking to hold for as long as possible so we are leaning toward a first cut at the June 5 announcement rather than at the April meeting based on data in hand.

Figure 1: Wages and unemployment rate
![](images/58ecbeb1d9dc309f7edd033fd5f060cd43974ea140935fb83c367de8ac718dcc.jpg)

We place a low probably on any action at the March meeting on the view that the amount of additional evidence on a sustained path to the 2.0% inflation target will still be too light to argue for more than a continued cautious on-hold stance. This week<sup>’</sup>s report was the last peek at labor market conditions in early 2024 and the Bank will have only one more CPI report for January before the March meeting. We expect 4Q23 real GDP growth released just before the March announcement will surprise the Bank to the upside.

Away from the favorable top line message of the January Labor Force Survey the report was more mixed. Employment growth was led by public sector employees and the January increase was entirely pinned to part-time work, which offset a notable decline in full-time. Total hours worked rose 1.1%oya and up 0.6%sa on the month, likely benefiting from the return to work of striking Quebec public sector workers, making for a constructive start to the current quarter. On average, job gains picked up over the last six months and over the last three months, entirely in the service sector. Goods sector performance was negative on both a six- and three-month trend in January.

## Some things we can’t control, part deux

In a speech this week Governor Macklem doubled down on Bank signaling that monetary policy is too blunt an instrument to address the current problem of national housing affordability. We continue to view this as a clear signal that the Bank intends to put less weight on persistently high shelter costs when considering the right time for the first rate cut. Governor Macklem noted that monetary policy can affect demand in the short run but can<sup>’</sup>t address long-running structural problems on the supply side, which are fundamental to affordability. A combination of low mortgage rates as the Central Bank reduced the policy to the zero lower bound (ZLB) during the height of the pandemic and a desire for more living space overwhelmed the supply of housing during 2020 and 2021. House prices rose more than 50% in two years<sup>’</sup> time. Then the Bank of Canada raised its policy rate to fight inflation and by increasing the cost of borrowing, eroded housing affordability in a different way. Years of housing supply shortages and the recent surge in immigrants means house prices have declined only modestly during the tightening cycle. The remarks complemented a special box in the January Monetary Policy Report that detailed the recent evolution of the CPI and the dynamics in the basket of shelter costs (Figure 2). One aspect that Governor Macklem steered clear of is the potential impact on inflation expectations. Mediumterm inflation expectations remain firmly anchored but ignoring unchecked shelter cost pressures risks de-anchoring of inflation expectations.

Figure 2: CPI-Shelter
![](images/e2c7898118a16a378e6b13a2250b5c916ba2116f3ad784b56986b69771f6baa7.jpg)

## Mexico

• Banxico opens door to cuts; we stick to -25bp in March

• January inflation rebounded to 4.9% on food; core CPI mixed

• December IP points to zero GDP growth in 4Q

• Reforms proposals raise concern; low odds of passing

Banxico was very clear in this week's statement that upside risks to inflation remain elevated, emphasizing the disappointing dynamics in non-core inflation, geopolitical risks, and climatic conditions. In line with recent inflation dynamics, the staff revised up its headline path for the short term with inflation now eyed at 4.7% in 1Q24 (from 4.3%) but left unchanged year-end inflation at 3.5%. On a more optimistic note, the staff revised core inflation down from 4.7% to 4.6%, still expecting this gauge at the same level as the headline by the end of the year.

The fact that the COPOM was unanimous in the first decision of the year is a clear acknowledgement that it remains important to address the complexities of the current inflation cycle; the board identified little progress on inflation expectations corrections and the risks associated with the recent spike in non-core inflation. But the board was clearly more comfortable with core inflation gains. Yet the most important additions to the February statement, in our view, were the following two guides.

First, it removed "it will be warranted to keep the policy rate in its current level for some time" and as we expected, said that “(the board) will start assessing the possibility of adjusting the reference rate subject to available information.<sup>”</sup> Second, the communique added that the board “will take into consideration the impact of the current restrictive monetary policy stance … on the inflation path on which monetary policy operates.<sup>”</sup>

Figure 1: Ex-ante real policy rate
![](images/206bd8baf86a7d620c96414656705d9ec3ebe3143cd89a01a3895eb2673a8244.jpg)
Source: Banxico and J.P. Morgan forecasts. Ex-ante policy rate range is Banxico's. Outlook updated Feb 1, 2024

In our view, this means the board is redirecting its focus to the real ex-ante rate—which stands at 7.5% now (Figure 1)— but remains aware of the challenging incoming information. Given its earlier guidance on this front and the inflation forecast targeting regime, we remain comfortable that Banxico will cut at the next meeting in March; however, if inflation dynamics deteriorate in February, particularly services, we would not be surprised if Banxico postponed the first cut to May.

While we think a significant interruption of core disinflation would stop the board from cutting 25bp in March, there was nothing in the statement that leads us to change our underlying view on the policy path ahead. We maintain that this will not be an aggressive easing cycle, that a properly restrictive rate is still needed in the face of structural and cyclical pressures on consumer prices, and that the end-2024 rate should thus remain elevated. We call for a policy rate at 9.75% by year-end and, in fact, risks remain skewed toward a higher policy rate by then. Our forecast has sequential core inflation remaining above 4%3m3m saar throughout 1H24 and the output gap becoming more positive as well over that time span, notwithstanding this week<sup>’</sup>s Dec IP data validating that GDP ran down to a halt last quarter. These would not be appropriate conditions to more than fine-tune an otherwise still-restrictive stance.

## Headline inflation up, core inflation mixed

We recently acknowledged that disinflation has gone a long way by now, but also that progress from here on will be painful. This week<sup>’</sup>s January CPI again reinforces that view. Headline and core prices both came close to our expectations at 0.89%m/m, nsa (vs. 0.92%) and 0.40% (vs. 0.39%), respectively. This pushed headline inflation back up to 4.9%oya from 4.7% in December and 4.3% in November. However, core inflation dropped 0.4%-pt to 4.8%.

Figure 2:Core nonfood and services %3m/3m, saar
![](images/6e1335bd8034918468e4cce6677b519c80a4103a8d49241112f457da22470b38.jpg)

Having said that, we are much more concerned about the sequential data than the trailing 12m inflation metrics, and here the plot thickens. Core prices increased roughly in line with our expectations, and price gains have moved closer to the pre-pandemic norm in the past couple of months. However, there remains a big, concerning divide between core goods and core services, and more so between core nonfood goods and services (Figure 2). Nonfood goods sequential inflation has seen a massive correction, going from a pandemic-induced high of about 9%3m3m annualized to a few tenths below 2% currently, roughly half its long-term norm. Several forces are seemingly at play, spanning from weak global manufactures demand to supply chain normalization and a strong domestic currency.

By contrast, however, services price gains<sup>’</sup> strong momentum is not yielding, with the January reading actually accelerating to 0.44%m/m, sa. The smoother 3m3m run-rate also remained firmly above 5%ar. Looking into the details, prices of services ex. housing and education are the most troublesome, with their 3m3m run-rate still close to 7%ar, even accounting for the recent helping hand from tourism-related prices. Removing these latter from the equation shows that while pressures in January of this year were not as strong as in the same month of 2023, price increases remain firmly above their norms across several sub-categories. Stubborn services inflation, contrary to goods inflation, is largely reflecting domestic conditions, most notably tight labor markets and rapid wage growth.

## Constitutional reforms

This week President López (AMLO) sent an ambitious reform agenda to Congress for discussion and potential (fasttrack) approval by April 30. In a nutshell, the 20-point plan comprises socioeconomic-policy proposals aimed at insulating the framework that incumbent Morena and AMLO have implemented (or attempted to) since 2018. The plan, as we see it, is intended to shape the institutional contours for the coming years to cement the long-term plans of the current administration. Although the approval of most of the items seems farfetched due to time constraints and the lack of a constitutional majority, just the discussion of the bills has already raised concerns considering the long-term implications for public finances, investment certainty, and USMCA challenges.

We have three main concerns. First is the risk of further undermining public finances if a new pension plan is approved, given an increasing reliance on non-recurrent revenues and discretionary spending at the cost of a narrow tax base and declining investment. Second, the intention to weaken (1) the Supreme Court of Justice and (2) the legislative— cutting the number of lawmakers would make it easier for the incumbent to reach a qualified majority—and (3) close autonomous entities would reduce accountability. Third, the attempt to ban at the constitutional level GMO corn imports

and hydraulic fracturing (fracking) and granting further protection to the State Electricity Company (CFE) given its strategic importance—at the cost of sidelining public private partnerships—will reinforce our view that pending USMCA panels on agriculture and energy will gain traction sooner rather than later.

All in all, while the bulk of the reforms are not expected to be approved, those that reach a thorough discussion beyond Congress<sup>’</sup> commissions will probably be watered down in order to gain endorsement from the opposition—particularly the pension reform and a new wage policy, to which the opposition has been more open. Pending further details, we regard the 20-point plan as short-sighted from an economic point of view. Given the ongoing fiscal deterioration (we expect the worst primary balance in more than 20 years for 2024), negative implications from a credit rating perspective could emerge sooner rather than later.

## Brazil

• Recent data suggest reacceleration of GDP growth at the beginning of 1Q...

• ...while CPI increased more than expected in January

• This bodes well with BCB<sup>’</sup>s cautious message

• Quieter Carnival week to be followed by political noise

The economic data this week suggest that growth probably rebounded at the same time the core CPI disinflation has stalled at the turn of the year. The January CPI report brought a concerning acceleration in core CPI, particularly in services prices (Figure 1). This renewed acceleration should be taken with a grain of salt as it is concentrated in some noisy prices. We keep seeing inflation well contained this year but acknowledge that the overall CPI picture has deteriorated.

Figure 1: Seasonally adjusted core CPI %3m/3m saar, black lines show the target range
![](images/55078ec38ac628ede6be3586e7edc38ad7066629ce24216a76271a6a7e9600e5.jpg)

Apart from retail sales, whose underwhelming December results must be taken with caution due to methodological changes and apparent excess seasonality not being captured, activity indicators are pointing to better results. This week the January services PMI rebounded to 53.1, the highest level in the last nine months, and services revenues recovered in both November and December (Figure 2). At the same time, credit standards are easing, which should reduce an significant drag on activity over time, consistent with our call for GDP growth to re-accelerate following a weak second half of 2023.

Figure 2: Activity indicators
![](images/bc7aedbab29eabee59fd41cf375e755716eb90456347b39f21603db3e2a3b1e5.jpg)

This economic backdrop seems consistent with BCB<sup>’</sup>s cautious message of late, expressed both in the minutes of the last COPOM meeting and in recent interviews of Board members. While BCB did not change the baseline scenarios for disinflation and GDP growth deceleration, it modified the characterization of risks, highlighting some upside risks to growth and inflation. With BCB seemingly not intending to rush the cycle, we see the recent communication reinforcing our case for 50bp cuts at the meetings during the first half of this year. We judge that the recent volatility in the external environment and the economic backdrop are in line with our baseline scenario of a 9.5% terminal rate, but we continue, though less so than before, to see asymmetric downside risks to this call.

One point made by BCB governor Campos Neto that touches on the balance of risks to this call is the importance of chasing the fiscal target, even if it is hard to achieve it (“Campos Neto: é importante o Brasil perseguir a meta fiscal, ainda que seja difícil atingir,<sup>”</sup> Estadão, February 7). We agree with this view, as noted in our recent forecast revision. Indeed our positive balance of risks on fiscal policy is in part related to signs that the discussion around a potential target change may not take place as early as we expect, being postponed to the second quarter of 2Q24. Improving mood, however, should not be taken for granted, as noise tends to increase as we edge closer to this year<sup>’</sup>s first bimonthly evaluation of fiscal accounts (March), and as sentiment is subject to political ebbs and flows.

## Services grew, but sales contracted in Dec

Core retail sales decreased 1.3%m/m, sa in December, an unusually weak print for the third December in a row, suggesting some lingering excess seasonality that is not being captured. Headline retail sales fell 1.1%m/m, sa or a more modest 0.4%m/m, sa when correcting for the methodological changes introduced last year. Meanwhile, services grew 0.3%m/m, sa in December, which with the upward revisions for November, left the indicator suggesting a rebound of the sector in 1Q24.

Figure 3: Brazil 4Q nowcaster
![](images/d3b51751e8c03755b1c48f894d814d9585c37a445d5c9e5b327740122ea07d3c.jpg)

Following these releases, last quarter<sup>’</sup>s nowcaster moved up one-tenth to 0.8%q/q, saar, above our 4Q GDP estimate, and this quarter<sup>’</sup>s projection went down to 2.3%, close to our call for a 2.4%q/q, saar expansion (Figures 3 and 4).

Figure 4: Brazil 1Q nowcaster
![](images/f15213cb13cb9ab61e13104cf9f1bde2dc5a99ce2d3117432c0f97fd74c82231.jpg)

## The lowest CAD in six years

The December current account deficit (CAD) was recorded at US\$5.8 billion, wider than our call of US\$5.3 billion but narrower than the consensus of US\$7.3 billion. CAD ended at 1.3% of GDP, driven by a rising trade balance to 3.7% of GDP, the strongest result since 2007. The main disappointment in December was the net outflow of US\$0.4 billion for foreign direct investment (FDI). Still, FDI flows were up 2.9% of GDP last year, keeping the basic balance (CAD plus FDI) up by around 1.5%-pts. We keep looking for 2024 CAD at 1.4% of GDP, rising to 1.6% next year, with FDI still running well above the current account deficit.

## Argentina

The Milei administration had to adjourn the session discussing the omnibus law article-by-article

• Setback in Congress forces the administration to recalculate the strategy ahead

• We estimate January CPI at 21%m/m, driving the over-year-ago CPI print to 255%oya

• January tax revenues boosted by foreign trade-related collection

Since the Milei administration took office, we have stressed two main challenges. First, governability. Second, the population<sup>’</sup>s tolerance for the adjustment (of both the fiscal accounts and relative prices). The first risk factor takes the spotlight after Milei<sup>’</sup>s administration had to adjourn the session discussing the omnibus law article-by-article, failing to obtain support from the opposition on the main articles. This is an unprecedented event; in recent memory no administration has had its first piece of legislation rejected.

Following the political setback, Milei's administration reinforced its commitment to the fiscal adjustment and to maintaining a reform agenda. Yet, the lack of Congressional support suggests the administration should recalculate its political strategy, opening the door to a more challenging period that could lead to increased volatility.

President Milei reacted immediately on social media, expressing that his government program was voted for by 56% of Argentinians, and "we are not willing to negotiate it with those who destroyed the country.<sup>”</sup> Milei asserted that "we will continue with our program with or without the support of the political leadership that destroyed our country.<sup>”</sup>

The setback creates a political bifurcation: the administration can insist on negotiating with governors (with additional concessions), or look to mobilize the voters that supported Milei<sup>’</sup>s proposals in the run-off, taking advantage of his still high approval rate. This second avenue would be consistent with the non-binding popular consultation avenue. But it should be noted that such a strategy per se does not solve the challenge posed by Congress. Moreover, courts of law could challenge the call. For now, it seems the dominant strategy would be to put the bill discussion on hold, and continue operating through Executive Decree. We thus face the first period of heightened political uncertainty, with potential implications for the FX gap in the near term.

## January CPI: Going down the ladder

We estimate January CPI at 21%m/m, driving the over-yearago CPI print to 255%oya. Our headline inflation forecast

(INDEC<sup>’</sup>s National CPI) is fairly in line with the 18.6%m/m suggested by the high-frequency data set tracked by EcoGo through January-end (note that in December the difference between the EcoGo tracker and the official data was 3.6%-pts, Table 1). The monthly inflation print would decelerate when compared to the 25.5% reported back in December, mainly on the back of the domestic demand contraction, though still at levels not reported since 1991. Indeed, weekly inflation has been decelerating lately, with January statistical carryover for February estimated at 4%-pts.

Regarding underlying inflation, we expect core CPI to decelerate to 20.8%m/m, with core ex. food CPI a tad above at 21.4%m/m. The breakdown by component shows core goods would have accelerated by 23%m/m, but core services “only<sup>”</sup> by 12%m/m, suggesting knock-on effects will linger. Food inflation would remain elevated (+19.7%m/m) on the back of the high statistical carryover left by December<sup>’</sup>s FX devaluation.

Seasonal and regulated prices would have contained inflation pressures in the month. We expect seasonal prices increased at 13.8%m/m, mainly explained by increases in fruits and vegetables (16.9%m/m and 14.7%, respectively). On the oth er side, regulated prices are also likely to print below the general level, at 15.8%m/m, amid energy tariffs freezing and despite the fuel and public transport price increase implemented on the month. Worth noting, regulated prices will probably start increasing more quickly in the coming months given the need to realign relative prices.

Figure 1: Headline CPI
![](images/d2ddc8f58d1bf2eb44249ec255059f3d3f605d6276831ab2b73f43a0c0c6d5bf.jpg)

We maintain our Dec-24 CPI unchanged at 210%oya while waiting to see how the realignment of relative prices develops. We expect inflation to remain very elevated in 1H24 amid the ongoing need to correct the sizable relative prices distortion promptly, a necessary condition for stabilization. Against this backdrop, we expect inflation to print in the double digits through 1Q23. Our forecast entertains a discrete FX jump in June 2024 , after which we expect the economy to stabilize at single-digit inflation levels if the stabilization

Manufacturing

program proves successful (Figure 1). Admittedly, the risks to our 2024 forecast are skewed to the upside if the FX convergence is delayed or the fiscal adjustment faces implementation challenges.

## Foreign-trade tax boost to Jan revenues

Monitoring the evolution of tax revenues will prove important ahead, particularly after the government<sup>’</sup>s political setback this week. The fiscal sacrifice from the removal of the fiscal chapter of the Omnibus bill is equivalent to 1.4% of GDP (plus 0.4% of GDP from not reversing the income tax reform), according to the administration<sup>’</sup>s figures (see note).

On an annual basis, real tax collection remained in negative territory in January, despite the government<sup>’</sup>s new measures (namely official FX devaluation and the increase in the PAIS tax on imports). Indeed, real tax collection declined by 4.3%oya in January (when deflating by the trailing 255%oya inflation rate). That said, on a sequential basis, January<sup>’</sup>s real tax collection (J.P. Morgan<sup>’</sup>s seasonal adjustment) leaped higher on the month, by 35%m/m, sa. The monthly jump should also be put into perspective, since it came on the heels of a 35% contraction accumulated in 2H23.

Zooming in on January<sup>’</sup>s details, foreign-trade tax collection jumped 270% monthly when adjusting for seasonality, explained by the FX devaluation, which drove export and import tax revenues higher by 446%m/m and 102%, sa, respectively. Going forward, the normalization of weather conditions and crop production will continue to support agriexports tax collection, but the ongoing appreciation in real terms of the official FX, and less supportive commodities prices could prove a drag.

The other good news is that collection arising from the “PAIS tax<sup>”</sup> jumped by 87%m/m, sa, as the government raised the applicable rate on imports to 17.5% (this change didn<sup>’</sup>t require Congressional approval). The administration estimates that the PAIS tax could add additional tax revenues of 0.8%-pt of GDP in full-year 2024. However, we emphasize the temporary nature of this tax, mainly because it should be eliminated once the exchange rate is unified (The IMF Staff notes the authorities are planning to fully unwind the PAIS tax by end-2024.)

The not so good news is that when excluding foreign trade-related taxes and the PAIS tax, total tax revenues declined by 14.4%oya in real terms (Figure 2). The real contraction was broad-based. Income tax dropped by 39%oya, driven by the elimination of the income tax for the fourth category. Note that the government has also put on hold the initiative to reinstate the income tax, which would imply sacrificing revenues of up to 0.4%-pt of GDP, according to its estimates, if the

administration does not insist on it. Meanwhile, consumptionrelated VAT and social security also printed in the red, down by 15.4%oya and 26.3%oya in real terms, respectively.

Figure 2: Total tax revenues
![](images/02d25ed363d0a7e83698fa674096e633d2b54179bbbaea29010da1b1897a1223.jpg)

In all, the January tax collection report unveils the challenges of fiscal adjustment, particularly on the revenue side, due to the temporary nature of some taxes (e.g., PAIS tax), the withdrawal of the fiscal package in Congress, and the expected economic activity plunge.

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P.Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Andeans

• Chile: Headline CPI logged 0.7%m/m, well above expectations

• Year-end headline CPI is revised to 3.52%oya

• We maintain as base case a new 100bp cut in April

• Colombia: Jan<sup>’</sup>24 CPI logged 0.92%m/m, driving the over-year-ago print to 8.35%oya

We project headline CPI to converge to 5.2%oya by Dec’24, below BanRep’s central scenario

## Chile: An upside inflation surprise

Inflation in Chile proved very volatile in the last months of 2023, with significant surprises vis-à-vis forecasts, though well distributed (to both the upside and the downside). The start of this year has proved no different. Admittedly, the fact that the new CPI index came into effect added uncertainty to this print (see note). Headline CPI logged 0.7%m/m (well above our 0.3%m/m projection and consensus of 0.4%). Annual CPI thus logged 3.8%oya, down from 3.9% in December.

When adjusting for seasonality (J.P. Morgan sa adjustment), headline CPI logged 0.4%m/m, sa, offsetting the 0.3% sa fall in December. Despite the leap higher, January<sup>’</sup>s pace does not deviate much from the 0.29% monthly average observed for the prior three months. In all, the last 3-m annualized momentum gained a bit, up to 4.3% sa, a bit above the inflation target range (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Chile headline CPI
![](images/291af6472be40bbb4deb1d949a26dec392fd241b877438a1f52282aba07a01c3.jpg)

On core, CPI ex. food and energy also printed +0.7%m/m, although we are still missing key information, as ex. volatile CPI, calculated by the CBC, will become available in the coming days.

In terms of forecasts, we revised the 2024 year-end headline CPI to 3.52%oya. When it comes to monetary policy, January<sup>’</sup>s upside surprise should not alter the Board<sup>’</sup>s decision to drive the policy rate lower, but adds uncertainty on the pace of convergence. Granted, the Board will be able to observe inflation<sup>’</sup>s performance in February, before assessing the size of the policy rate cut in early April. While it is still early, we forecast a low February CPI print, below 0.1%m/m. We thus maintain as base case a new 100bp cut in April. That said, we reinforce our view that terminal rate will not be below 4.5%.

## Colombia: Sequential core CPI decelerated

In Colombia, December CPI logged 0.92%m/m, below expectations (J.P. Morgan: 0.99%) and the 1.78% in January 2023. Annual inflation thus continued to decelerate, down to 8.35%oya, from 9.28%oya in December. The main category explaining the forecast error was food, which despite El Niño conditions continued to gain moderately, adding 9bp to headline CPI. On its side, ex.food CPI logged just 1bp above our forecast.

When adjusting for seasonality, headline CPI logged 0.40%m/m, sa, decelerating strongly compared to the prior 3-month average of 0.55%. The 3-month annualized pace eased to 5.91%, still well above the target range ceiling, but 88bp below the prior print. The gap between the last 12-m and the 3m ar rates aligns to our base case of a sustained downward trend for the annual CPI ahead.

Regarding underlying inflation, core CPI as tracked by Ban-Rep (ex. food & regulated) gained 0.73%m/m, again a material deceleration compared to Jan<sup>’</sup>23 (1.33%). Annual core CPI decelerated to 7.81%oya, from 8.43% in December. Equally relevant, the seasonally adjusted pace of 0.4%m/m, sa is the first significant sequential deceleration signal of the last six months. This is consistent with a 4.92% annualized level, which compares to 6.97% in the prior three months (Figure 2). However, a more detailed breakdown of the core CPI print continues to show the different behavior of goods and services. The last 3m annualized core goods pace actually sits below the 2015-19 average, but core services continue to prove sticky, actually accelerating by a tad.

Figure 2: Colombia headline ex.food and regulated CPI % , sa by J.P. Morgan
![](images/df9a66e05a19eb75d5aa06b64dd48d727cd08bee873769006eedf3f8d0ccf2ca.jpg)
Source: DANE and J.P. Morgan

CPI

Week of February 12 - 16

In terms of the CPI forecast, we maintain our central scenario of annual headline CPI easing ahead to 5.2%oya. Forecast uncertainty remains elevated, particularly for food and electricity prices on El Niño. Our projection remains below the recent revision of the Staff, as discussed in a recent note (see note). We also maintain the policy rate converging to 8.5% by December, with 6.25% the terminal level (see note).

The author wishes to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, J.P.Morgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## United Kingdom

Revised data show the unemployment rate has been falling

• Near-term growth prospects are mixed, but green shoots appear

• Signs of a turn in the housing market

• Downside risk to GDP next week; temporary pickup in inflation

Views on 1H24 growth prospects have become more polarized. The pessimistic view sees an economy that has been stagnating for a while and little reason why the picture should improve with monetary policy still restrictive. This view was highlighted by the MPC<sup>’</sup>s Swati Dhingra earlier this week, warning that the UK could be on the verge of a “profound<sup>”</sup> negative hit. In contrast, others appear more optimistic, noting that the conditions for a growth upturn are falling into place. Sarah Breeden this week expressed greater confidence that “downside risks to demand are less likely to materialize<sup>”</sup> while Catherine Mann expects “somewhat stronger<sup>”</sup> demand.

Our forecast, in the near term at least, has been on the more optimistic side in looking for a clear step up in growth in 1H24. That is largely due to strength in household real income growth and a confidence boost from falling inflation. More recently we factor in easier financial conditions and the scope for a further fiscal easing at the March Budget. Of course it is difficult to have confidence in this view when the available hard data through to late last year are still very weak. However, we do see signs in some of the recent softer indicators that a growth upturn may be in the offing:

Relative to the (since revised higher) low of 46.2 last August, the PMI in January stood at 52.5 with future output running above its average. The PMI has been less reliable in gauging the absolute pace of growth lately, but may be providing an important signal about better growth momentum. The Lloyds business confidence barometer has been high and rising through to January, while the Gfk<sup>’</sup>s consumer confidence measure is low but is also now trending higher into this year.

There are signs of a turn in the housing market. Activity remains depressed, but the balance on new buyer enquiries in the January RICS survey released this week turned positive for the first time since April 2022 (Figure 1). Agreed and expected sales are up sharply from last summer<sup>’</sup>s lows, and the sales to stocks ratio - which correlates well with the underlying price trend in the market - appears to corroborate the annualized 7.4% surge in the Halifax and Nationwide price indices in January, measured on a 3m/3m basis.

Figure 1: RICS survey - new buyer enquiries % balance, sa
![](images/5acd1aaeb5086986f0d95358b9c9d699b391e7365040fd85ecca830f11b6a5bc.jpg)
Source: RICS, J.P. Morgan

The improvement in housing likely reflects the recent drop in new mortgage rates, with two year fixed rates this week falling another 30bps in January and 160bps lower than the peak last summer (Figure 2). An upswing in housing does not necessarily imply a large direct positive contribution to GDP. And of course many existing borrowers have yet to roll off onto what will be higher rates. But the indirect effects from a potential housing market upturn could be important as a barometer of broader confidence and spending attitudes.

Figure 2: Average effective mortgage rates <sup>Start</sup> <sup>of</sup> % tightening
![](images/c27fa1a172af50b526405a2c205b9a330b6fe97a2463c2ea6c252edb53b13ab3.jpg)
Source: BoE, J.P. Morgan

The REC survey for January is one of the more timely surveys that runs counter to the upside risk highlighted above. Permanent placements dropped back by over two points to 43.4 and at face value flags risks of a contraction in employment. Faith in the REC at the moment is waning, however, as there has been little corroboration of this in other indicators such as the PMI, DMP, BCC and payrolls data. Moreover, ONS labor market revisions this week show the unemployment rate has been falling recently, whereas the REC staff availability reading has continued to flag a rising jobless rate. Despite the REC having also been pointing to 3-4% wage growth for over a year now, early hints about 2024 pay are coming in closer to 5%.

The interesting question from here is whether growth will turn and how “strong<sup>”</sup> it could plausibly get this year. So far, BoE pushback on an early easing has mostly been about persistence in underlying inflation. The Bank is still expecting growth to run at a very weak average annualized pace of 0.4% in 1H24. The idea of a “no-landing<sup>”</sup> might not be applicable for an economy that has already been flatlining. But the BoE has set the bar quite low for an upside surprise to growth this year, and a beat to its forecast could start a debate about an unanticipated “take-off<sup>”</sup> scenario. Near term growth momentum could hence play a more active role in influencing perceptions about where inflation will ultimately settle, and is ready to start cutting.

## ONS revisions show a falling u-rate

From next Tuesday, the ONS will be reinstating its Labour Force Survey, replacing the experimental series it has temporarily been publishing since October that made use of very limited information. The decision to reinstate the LFS reflects progress made in boosting survey response rates. Nevertheless, the ONS continues to emphasize that the data should be interpreted with caution until further improvements are implemented. The data also incorporate latest population estimates and show the reweighting impact this has had on elements of the LFS.

In advance of next Tuesday<sup>’</sup>s release, the ONS has published indicative estimates of what the revised data will look like for the period from September 2022 to November 2023. We discuss the details below, but the main takeaways are 1) that the unemployment rate is estimated to be lower than the experimental data had suggested and 2) more significantly, that the unemployment rate has been on a downward trajectory since July, despite a stagnant economy. This appears as a challenge to the BoE<sup>’</sup>s assumption that the labor market has been loosening recently.

Following falling response rates to its LFS survey – as a result of shifting to telephone interviews during the pandemic – the ONS recently opted to resume face-to-face interviews with the intention of boosting the sample size. The ONS now seems more comfortable with the estimates of the data from September 2023, but nevertheless has cautioned that the data should be read alongside a broader set of indicators. The long-term solution is to shift to a new online-based survey (called the TLFS). After initially indicating this would be available in the spring, the ONS has stated that this will now be published this September.

The indicative estimates follow upward revisions to estimates of the population growth announced last week. This has pushed up the estimated number of employed, unemployed and those inactive. But the impact of the rates of these vari-

ables is more nuanced. The population reweighting means the unemployment rate has seen only a small 0.1%-pt upward revision on average from September 2022 to November 2023, at least when compared to the original series that was discontinued after the July 2023 release (see orange vs. blue line on the chart below). However, the revision makes no difference to the period just before the suspension of the LFS, where the unemployment rate for July 2023 remains at 4.3%. And the latest data, to November 2023, show the unemployment rate lower than estimated by the temporary experimental data (Figure 3).

Figure 3: UK unemployment rate %, sa, ILO
![](images/339fddb092cb39a59adbf4d3309ac36c1eea026f77fb5ade9b35f9ba65b6d69d.jpg)

The most interesting development is in that latest period. Instead of flatlining at 4.2% for the past five months, as suggested by the experimental data, the latest indicative estimates show the unemployment rate lower and on a falling trajectory. The unemployment rate drops at a pace of one-tenth per month to stand at 3.9% as of November. This is very different to the message of a rising jobless rate implied by the REC survey and, at face value, indicates a tightening labor market despite GDP having stagnated during this period. This is explained by the participation rate falling, rather than a rising employment rate.

The ONS has said that the downward revision to the participation rate was concentrated in the 16-24 age group and is more skewed towards females. As we have pointed out in the past, news that population growth is stronger than before needs to be taken alongside how its composition has changed in order to understand how the labor force has been affected. One example is students who return from COVID: this boosts the population, but likely lowers the average participation rate and average hours worked. So, while the population is now estimated to be larger, the overall boost to the labor supply is not as great.

Does a lower unemployment rate imply a tighter labour market than before? Not necessarily, as inflation and survey based indicators of tightness haven<sup>’</sup>t changed over this period.

A lower unemployment rate may therefore imply a lower equilibrium rate than before. This is the same way the BoE tends to read revisions to GDP. The new data may have a bearing on the BoE<sup>’</sup>s recently published view on potential growth (which projected an improvement to above 1%). But here the conclusion is mixed. While the BoE will need to incorporate the upward revisions to population growth projections, there may be an offsetting impact from a lower participation rate (and lower productivity).

Where the revisions appear more significant is in the trajectory of the unemployment rate. The BoE last week assumed this would drift gently upward over time (from from 4.2% to 4.6% by year end). But this assumption may now be challenged by the fact that the most recent data show the unemployment rate instead falling 0.4%-pts over the past four months (from 4.3% to 3.9%). While the BoE may argue that falling vacancies still point to a cooling labor market, the revised trajectory of the unemployment rate should at least question the degree to which that is happening. And it may also help to explain the resilience in some indicators of wage growth.

The ONS unemployment data are set to be revised down to 3.9%through to November, following the announcement this week. Our best guess is that this level persists into December, although the recent downward trajectory implies there could be a further decline to 3.8%. Base effects will pull annual rates of average earnings growth down sharply in December. But we look for non-bonus pay to rise 0.4%m/m after the prior month<sup>’</sup>s 0.6%. These data are volatile at the moment but this forecast would be consistent with a near 5% annualized 3m/3m pace on earnings growth.

<sup>1.</sup> CPI ex food, energy, alcohol, and tobacco.

Inflation is likely to show a temporary pickup in January, reflecting a rise in regulated domestic energy bills. We expect core inflation to be broadly steady, with offsetting moves in core goods (downward) and services (upward). There may be some upside risk to goods judging by the recent shift higher in the PMI<sup>’</sup>s manufacturing pricing readings, although it is probably too early to expect a notable impact from Red Sea disruptions. The shift higher in annual services inflation from 6.4% to 6.7%oya relates to base effects from last year (a broad based downside surprise that we expect won<sup>’</sup>t be repeated). Over the month, we expect seasonally adjusted ser vices prices to be flat. This reflects an assumed unwind from a large spike in transport services in December. Even excluding this, we expect a relatively soft 0.3%m/m, sa rise. The reason for that is because of the extreme softness shown in the core services basket this time last year. While would do not expect this to fully be repeated, there may have been a seasonal element to last year<sup>’</sup>s surprise that could be at play again this January. The weights will also be updated. We expect the weight on energy to come down slightly, due to the cut in bills last July. We think this will act to lift inflation slightly, compared to a counterfactual of unchanged weights, given that energy bills are expected to fall later this year.

The large drop in December retail sales, together with the firmer GDP outturn from November which may unwind, points to at least a small decline in December GDP. This would likely produce a 0.0% on 4Q GDP, although the number could come in lower and show a small negative. This would mean there could be two consecutive quarters of negative growth shown during 2H24. As we have argued previously, however, any other read of the data does not point to a recession dynamic at please.

## Sweden and Norway

• Norway inflation below Norges Bank<sup>’</sup>s forecast

• Swedish January prices returning to normal pattern

• Riksbank makes dovish pivot

The Swedish GDP indicator was marginally up in 4Q23, 0.4%q/q, saar, following contractions in the previous two quarters. The monthly GDP indicator for December contracted 0.3%m/m (-0.1%oya), and as expected, previous months were revised down. No details were reported but the statistics office noted that net exports contributed to the increase while domestic demand contracted.

The indicator has recently sent conflicting signals relating to survey data, and the ETI also remained at gloomy levels in January, despite a 5.8pts increase. Whilst encouraging, a reading below 90 should be interpreted as “growth much below normal<sup>”</sup> (below 100 is “weaker than normal<sup>”</sup>). According to our models, this is consistent with GDP contracting -0.7%q/q, saar, below our expectation of flat activity in 1Q24. The Composite PMI was also a bit more upbeat in January, with the composite index rising to 50.5, consistent with GDP only marginally down at 0.1%q/q, saar. While the Swedish manufacturing PMI was down 1.7pts to 41.1 (with details also being quite negative), the services PMI moved up 1.5pts to 51.8. Noteworthy, delivery times lengthened, likely in response to developments in the Red Sea, but prices still fell. For both the services and manufacturing PMI prices indicate significant disinflation ahead.

The Riksbank unanimously decided to keep the policy rate on hold at 4.0% as widely expected. The focus was instead on forward guidance, which showed a clear dovish pivot. The Board assessed that a cut could come sooner than indicated in November, perhaps already “during the first half of the year<sup>”</sup>. In response to this, we have pulled forward our expectations of a first cut from June to May. The Board also decided to increase the pace of government bond sales from SEK 5bn to SEK 6.5bn/month.

The January minutes were much more dovish compared to November, but on the margin less so than the press conference. The members generally expressed optimism about the inflation outlook, although they were not prepared to fully declare victory over inflation yet. All members were also concerned about a potentially weaker SEK, pushing back on speculation of a cut in March.

There is some uncertainty about the upcoming January inflation print. Normally, core prices decline significantly in January, but in the past couple of years this pattern has been broken, driven by stronger-than-normal out-turns for food,

recreation/culture, restaurant/hotels and furnishing. But, we expect a return to “normal<sup>”</sup> this year. Food prices according to independent price-tracker Matpriskollen support this, while data out of Norway this week in contrast hint of a lingering post-pandemic seasonal pattern. Overall, we expect core prices to fall 0.6%m/m (4.2%oya, Riksbank: 4.5%). Risk is to the upside, partly for the reasons outlined above, partly due to a weaker SEK in January and supply chain disruptions. Basket effects are roughly balanced.

Figure 1: Sweden core inflation vs. Western Europe
![](images/67fe1e67ec90bfdd32e6f462871e5fe48ff14b178f3dbff93486baa035cf3c99.jpg)

## Norway: Core CPI below NB forecast

The Norwegian PMI to a large extent mirrored the Swedish PMI. The headline and output indices fell 0.9pts to 50.7 and 0.7pts to 49.3, respectively. New orders and employment fell while delivery times soared. In contrast to the Swedish PMI, however, the price component slightly increased and inventories fell.

Core prices were flat in January, with the oya rate falling 0.2%-pt to 5.3%. Whilst higher than expectations, it remained lower than Norges Bank<sup>’</sup>s forecast. A changed seasonal pattern in e.g. food, hospitality and recreation caused the upside surprise. This also led to firmer domestic inflation. We do, however, think this was a temporary effect as leading indicators point to significant disinflation ahead (Figure 2). We still expect a first cut in June, but risk is skewed toward a later move.

Figure 2: Domestic core inflation
![](images/f4e5e06e42d3f44d9cfbad92bb82cbc692e77eeb1e2dd40717ebbd58dd128f1a.jpg)

## Emerging Europe

• CNB speeds up the pace of easing

• NBP stays uneventful

• Hungary: Inflation returns to the tolerance band

• Türkiye: Inflation momentum picked up in January

The CNB cut the policy rate by 50bps to 6.25% (Figure 1), a deeper cut than the 25bps expected by the analyst consensus (ourselves included), but in line with market pricing. The decision was backed by 6 (out of 7) board members, with a dovish dissenter pushing for 75bps. At this point our best guess is that the dissenter is either Deputy Governor Jan Frait, who explicitly mentioned the possibility of “more than 50<sup>”</sup> publicly, or alternatively Tomas Holub .

Figure 1: CNB interest rate corridor
![](images/3df26b065afe94f67b30d2fcaa5724ad7f842361d5bc740ff241ce2e037a0ecc.jpg)

The statement explains the decision with the decline in inflation, which the central bank estimates slowed to 3%oya in January (the upper-limit of the target band), from 6.9% in December. This is not news though, the inflation forecast is basically the same as before. It seems the board felt more reassured a potential flare up of inflation in January is not as likely. The trend weakness in the exchange rate is not mentioned, and in the press conference, Governor Michl had the opportunity to clarify that FX was not an important part of the decision, a surprise relative to our expectations (both that it didn<sup>’</sup>t matter and that he said it).

Judging by the content of the statement, nothing seems to have changed. The board assesses risks to the forecast as “broadly inflationary<sup>”</sup>, the materialization of such risks implying inflation falling relative to previous years “but not close to the 2% target<sup>”</sup>. Therefore the board “considers it necessary to persist with tight monetary policy and approach further rate cuts with caution<sup>”</sup>. The text also highlights risks of “higher-than-expected inertia in services prices and a halt in tradeables disinflation<sup>”</sup> or the risk of increased lending activity in the housing market which “could lead to renewed highly positive contributions of imputed rent to core inflation<sup>”</sup>.

We have argued a more cautious 25bp cut would have been on the margin preferable at this point, especially in the context of trend FX weakness, a trend which predictably was reinforced after the decision. But considering the high starting point for interest rates, neither a 25bp or a 50bp cut would<sup>’</sup>ve been a terrible mistake, as in either case the board can steer rates towards any desired terminal rate over the next 12-18 months. So we don<sup>’</sup>t think this decision changes the direction of travel for rates meaningfully, but the FX risks to the inflation forecast will be growing from here as the deviation from the CNB forecast is already sizeable. We maintain our call for rate cuts of 50bps in March and May, to be followed by a reduction in the pace to 25bps steps as signs of inflation stickiness become more evident in the data, with the policy rate ending 2024 at 4% (previously 4.25%).

## NBP: Rates on hold

For a fourth consecutive time, the NBP decided to keep the policy rate unchanged at 5.25%, as universally expected (Figure 2). The statement portrays the inflation outlook favorably in the near term, with a significant decline expected at the start of 2024 (we expect 3.4% in 1Q24, down from 6.2% in 4Q23), but with plenty of uncertainty in the remainder of the year, due to potential decisions about the normalization of food VAT and adjustment of energy price caps. The text also argues domestic demand will be stimulated in 2024 by increases in public wages sponsored by the new Government. The phrase “the NBP may intervene in the FX market<sup>”</sup> was maintained, but overall guidance remains vague.

![](images/0afbe32ec240af50b54bc3addcb886c8388c01ca427e9358db2daf41af87fec4.jpg)

We expect two small cuts towards the end of the year. We assume that food VAT will be normalized in April, but mid year, energy tariffs will be adjusted marginally only, which means headline inflation will likely end the year sub 5%, and the NBP will have some room to cut. However, we see core CPI stuck around 4.6% at the end of 2024, and hence ,that space is limited. In a context of easing by both the Fed and the ECB, as well as regional peers (CNB, NBH), we expect the NBP will tweak rates lower marginally in 4Q, in two steps of 25bps, to 5.25% at year-end.

## Hungary: CPI back to tolerance band

In January Hungary<sup>’</sup>s consumer prices were up 0.7%m/m, with a decline in headline CPI to 3.8%oya (Figure 3), from 5.5%. This marks a return to within the NBH<sup>’</sup>s tolerance band (3%oya +/- 1%-pt). The data were below market expectations for 4.3%, but near our 3.9% call.

Figure 3: Hungary inflation
![](images/aadccf530d26fa5dfed6fe49a110974f9788d9d20b9bf11dc13f6c1d82a24a20.jpg)

Food prices were up 1.3%m/m, stronger than expected (0.6%m/m) for the first time in a while, driven mainly by raw food items (3%), especially vegetables, but processed food inflation was also positive (0.5%). Seasonally adjusted, we estimate food prices were up 1.4%m/m, which is the first positive reading since April 2023, possibly the first signs of some normalization in food inflation. Alcohol and tobacco prices were up 1.0%m/m (much less than our expected 2.7%). Fuel prices fell 0.5% on the month, in marked deviation from the surveys (+2.5%), leading to the over-year-ago rate falling to -11.9%oya. Electricity tariffs were flat but natural gas tariffs increased 1.6%m/m as a bigger share of consumption was done at the higher price tier. As result, and with some increase in motorway tolls, regulated prices on the whole were up 1.1%.

The NBH<sup>’</sup>s main metric of core inflation was up 0.4%m/m in January, leading to a decline to 6.1%oya from 7.6%, in line with our forecast. Our favorite measure, which in addition excludes processed food, was up just 0.35%m/m, much below our expectation for 0.6%m/m, and hence fell in annual terms to 8.1%oya, from 9.5%. We estimate that core CPI was up 0.5%m/m in seasonally adjusted terms, or 6% in annualized terms, a bit lower than in December (7.4%m/m saar), but still higher than the lowest point achieved in July-November, when core momentum was oscillating between 3.5-5.5%m/m saar. In particular, core service prices continued to move at a pace similar to previous months, elevated around 8%m/m saar.

With inflation likely to remain sub 4.5% in the next few months, and with political tail risks contained, we think the NBH has space to return to the idea of 100bps steps already this month (cutting to 9%). However, as we see both headline and core inflation close to 5% at the end of the year, we think the NBH will have to gradually slow the pace of cuts through the year, to an expected end-2024 rate of 5.25%.

## Türkiye: Upside risks to inflation outlook

Türkiye January CPI came in at 6.7% m/m nsa, close to our expectation of 6.8% and higher than the market consensus of 6.5%. CPI inflation increased slightly to 64.9% oya in January, from 64.8% in December. Food prices rose 5.2% m/m, pushing annual food inflation down to 69.7% oya in January, from 72%. Services prices rose a high 12.1% m/m in January, owing to a higher-than-expected minimum wage hike of 49%. Over-year-ago services inflation fell 1%pt to 89.7% thanks to favourable base effects. We see the over-year-ago disinflation process post May 2024 as more challenging as services inflation tends to remain sticky. Core goods prices rose 3.6% m/m, pushing up over-year-ago inflation to 53.4% in January, driven by durable goods prices due to the accelerated TRY depreciation in January. As a result, core prices rose a high 7.6% m/m, leading oya core inflation to remain elevated at 70.5% in January.

Core inflation momentum (3m change, saar, %) picked up in January mainly due to services prices as we expected. Core goods inflation momentum is also marginally up due to the accelerated TRY depreciation in January. We expect core inflation to remain elevated in 1Q24 owing to second-round effects from wage hikes, administered price hikes and tax adjustments indexed to realised inflation.

Headline over-year-ago inflation is set to rise towards 73% in May 2024 owing to higher-than-expected wage hikes and unfavourable base effects driven by natural gas prices. We maintain our year-end inflation forecast at 42.2%, but see upside risks due to sticky services inflation. We will monitor to what extent real appreciation of lira and the economic slowdown will offset the effects of realised wage hikes coupled with expected electricity and natural gas price hikes after the March municipal elections.

We maintain our year-end policy rate forecast of 45%, with the risks for higher. We do not rule out further tightening in coming months by the CBRT depending on inflation prints in February and March. That said, according to the CBRT\`s press release yesterday, the newly appointed Governor has signalled that the CBRT is not planning to hike the policy rate at this stage. Instead, the CBRT pledged to keep the policy rate high for longer. We believe that the CBRT will keep the door open for further rate hikes in its forward guidance in the Inflation Report Press Briefing on Thursday, in case the inflation outlook deteriorates in February and March. We feel more confident now that the CBRT is likely to keep its policy

Israel:

rate at 45% (or higher) this year without any premature rate cuts as the existing MPC is more sensitive to the inflation outlook.

On hold: 16.0%

The meeting is likely to be uneventful, as the tightening cycle is certainly over — in fact, the recent drop in CPI momentum and signs of cooling of economic activity suggest that the CBR may have over-tightened policy — but not enough time has passed since the last hike to start a serious discussion of policy easing yet.

## South Africa

• Manufacturing output down 1.7%m/m sa in December

• Full quarter IP still tracks a slight positive contribution to GDP

• SONA suggests continuation and improvement of grants

We expect an uneven IP recovery will underpin the 1.5%q/q ar GDP gain we estimate in 4Q. Sequential tracking into November suggests mining output more than regained the 4.2%q/q ar it lost in 3Q with a 12.7% rise in 4Q. Next week<sup>’</sup>s December data should provide a full view of mining production last quarter, but we see balanced risks to overall growth. On the contrary, the recovery momentum in manufacturing output looks weak as we track a 0.6% gain for the quarter, following a 5% contraction in 3Q.

The data suggest the manufacturing sector has been impacted most by the ongoing logistical challenges at the ports. In December, output declined by 1.7%m/m sa as motor parts nose-dived by 28.4% (from +3.7%), and electrical machinery by 8.1% (+1.7%). These products are mostly shipped via rail and this mirrors the messaging from the external activity report for December where both these categories declined to shrink overall trade surplus. Even so, the poor December manufacturing performance comes against a strong 1.2% gain in November, leaving the full quarter mildly negative. That said, the sector grew a tepid 0.4%y/y last year, with output yet to recover to pre-pandemic levels.

Figure 1: South Africa - Manufacturing output
![](images/0dd746d577f65a1b300be4214528db3fa4bd371af71b829bf3ac1984e79021c5.jpg)
Source: StatsSA, J.P Morgan calculations

Separately, the state of the nation (SONA) address delivered few surprises nor contained major popular announcements, despite impending elections. The president stressed the gains achieved over the last three decades rather than more recent developments. That said, the address signalled that the SRD grant will be extended and improved, with an update expected in the upcoming Budget. Moderate revisions to the grant structure need not increase the expenditure envelope as unallocated reserves should provide sufficient space. Even so, we expect a moderate fiscal slippage on both revenue and expenditure with a 5.1% deficit (Treasury: 4.3%) in the upcoming fiscal year FY23/24.
