## Australia and New Zealand

RBA left rates on hold this week, as widely expected

• But the statement referenced that further tightening was possible

• We expect Australia<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate to hold at 3.9% in January

NZ’s labor market was stronger than expected in 4Q23, the jobless rate only moved up 0.1%

The RBA board left the cash rate unchanged at 4.35% at this week<sup>’</sup>s decision, as widely expected. This was the first meeting in which the decision statement was written by the board rather than the Governor, with the quarterly SoMP document delivered alongside, among other procedural changes discussed below. This required a number of presentational tweaks, but the tone carried on much of the message from December, which is notable for the absence of change relative to the global evolution over the last two months, and compared to what the market has seemed to expect after the last CPI result.

We had expected the statement to moderate the hiking guidance toward a more neutral language appropriate for a highhold scenario, while emphasizing that achievement of the target is some way off. The revamped guidance achieves that. The clause "whether further tightening … is required" has been removed, but the final paragraph reiterates that it "will be some time yet before inflation is sustainably in the target range. The path of interest rates that will best ensure that … will depend on the data … and a further increase in interest rates cannot be ruled out." The "path" of course covers hold and cut scenarios too, but if policy were to move at all near term, the emphasis is clearly still on tightening.

Figure 1: RBA SoMP headline inflation forecasts
![](images/f6f39181a1430c00608b8e26681f5576838a407fe28dce83c65c1e4d08ec5ed3.jpg)

The SoMP forecasts were lowered as expected, with headline inflation now projected to be 3.2% in Dec-24, previously 3.5%, and core is at 3.1%, previously 3.3%. This is a downgrade vs the November SoMP, but most of the forecasts are close to those the staff delivered back in August (Figure 1).

In the context of last week<sup>’</sup>s seemingly good news on inflation for 4Q, the board's interpretation of the data was quite sober, remarking on the "gradual" decline in services inflation. Commentary on the labor market also had a somewhat hawkish inflexion as the board presented its new dual mandate in 'spot' terms: arguing that the labor market remains "tighter than is consistent with full employment and inflation at target."

While growth moderation is forecast (unemployment to rise 0.5%-pt), as it stands the economy is not yet in a sustainable place regarding the inflation constraint. This is a reminder that maintenance of the current policy stance, and downward trend in inflation is required before the rate cut conversation can get off the ground. We have expected the RBA to lag other DM central banks in easing, and also being less willing to pre-emptively canvass the case for cuts this year. This week's commentary appears consistent with that.

## Australian jobless rate holding firm

The stability in Australia<sup>’</sup>s unemployment rate in December (unchanged at 3.9%) masked some large swings in the other survey details with participation falling 0.5%-pts (Figure 2) and employment growth plunging 65K. Participation had been very strong in prior months and the ABS flagged seasonal abnormalities and flood/cyclone related distortions may have influenced collection patterns in Queensland, imparting a downward bias on labor supply. For this reason we forecast a degree of mean reversion in participation (67%) and a rebound in employment (+50K) in the upcoming print, though expect stability in the jobless rate at 3.9%.

Figure 2: Australian labor force participation rate
![](images/64443d81d476f82a6f4ffad8f7789078b5abfea3c64922f9ed9617ee72697711.jpg)

## NZ labor market surprisingly strong

The NZ unemployment rate ticked up another tenth in 4Q, to 4.0%. This is the highest level in two and a half years, and in line with the pre-pandemic (late 2019) jumping-off point. But the data fell short of consensus expectations for a larger increase (to 4.3%) and to a lesser extent, our forecast (4.1%). Employment growth was moderate and in line with our expectation at 0.4%q/q, below working age population growth, and this was associated with weaker-than-expected participation which dipped slightly to 71.9%. The underutilization rate revealed more of the underemployment adjustment underway, and so moved comparatively more, with a 3-tenth increase to 10.7%, also the highest level since mid-21.

Average hourly earnings numbers cooled (+0.5%q/q from +2%q/q) which is not surprising as this is a noisy indicator which has mostly moved inversely to hours through the pandemic. The official LCI (private ex-overtime) wage figure was firmer than expected at 1.0%q/q, but still maintained a decelerating trend such that the %oya rate fell another 2 tenths to 3.9%, from a peak of 4.5%. We expect this measure to be running in the mid-3s by mid-24.

The business survey and job vacancy metrics have shown a significant rebalancing of labor supply relative to demand in NZ over the last year (Figure 3), which is adding capacity and helping to bring inflation down. The details of the 4Q CPI report similarly showed the proportion of items annualizing at above 2%, 3% and 4% was back at pre-pandemic levels, which is to be expected as the unemployment rate moves back to pre-pandemic levels. Still, the RBNZ has, in its limit ed communications of recent months, been reluctant to extrapolate the trajectory of the data, which is significant since the real OCR should approach 3% in 2H24 in the absence of rate cuts.

The RBNZ<sup>’</sup>s “least regrets<sup>”</sup> policy framework seems set up for an abrupt shift from managing too-high inflation, to managing downside risks if the restrictive stance isn<sup>’</sup>t wound back into 2H24. In our view, the steps in this transition are actually being taken in the background, e.g., in introducing mortgage debt to income restrictions, which mitigates the house price spillovers from a lower cash rate, even if recent commentary is still emphasizing above-target <sup>‘</sup>spot<sup>’</sup> inflation.

Figure 3: NZ QSBO labour constraints and wage growth %oya, lagged 2Q
![](images/cea88885b3cf99850b2cdd932a237f1fc1852e82777558d49b3ae74c6247af3c.jpg)

## Greater China

• China: CPI deflation intensified in January on food prices and LNY seasonality

• Credit report beat expectations; bank loans picked up amid growth-supportive policies

• FX reserves fell to \$3219bn

• Taiwan: January CPI inflation eased to 1.79%oya, due partly to LNY base effect

● Exports modestly below expectations; strong imports hint at upcoming solid export trend

China<sup>’</sup>s CPI deflation intensified in January: CPI fell 0.8%oya (the index was down 0.4%m/m sa, Figure 1). Food prices dragged on headline CPI, falling 1.6% m/m sa (or down 5.9%oya), with easing in pork prices (down 17.3%oya) and vegetable prices (down 12.7%oya). Vehicle fuel prices also fell 1.0% m/m nsa (or down 0.1%oya) in January. Core CPI inflation eased further, rising 0.4%oya in January (vs. 0.6%oya in December), or falling 0.1%m/m sa. While the easing in the January CPI print partly reflects Lunar New Year seasonality (as the LNY holiday fell in January last year and February this year), the January CPI data still look soft, even compared with past experience with a similar seasonal factor for the month of January.

Figure 1: China CPI trends
![](images/9b3d0ee24b6ff20508d442997bac533c589f39dd9d7c37b9fd8fd0487768664d.jpg)

PPI stayed in deflation territory, but stabilized somewhat, and headline PPI fell 2.5%oya in January (vs. -2.7%oya in December) and stayed flat in % m/m sa terms. Energy-related PPI components eased modestly, along with global energy prices. Producer prices for domestic industrial materials stabilized, while downstream consumer goods remained sluggish.

The generally weak pricing trend is dragging on nominal GDP growth. This causes concerns about discrepancies between policymakers<sup>’</sup> interpretation of macro data (with a focus on real GDP growth) and industry/corporate-level performance, as weaker nominal growth amid deflation pressure drags on corporate revenue, cash flow and profits, exerting further stress on private corporate confidence.

We expect deflation to end, but low inflation to stay in 2024 with the bottoming out of global commodity and domestic pork prices, though policy support shifting from supporting the supply side to the demand side is not visible yet. Possible policy measures to deal with demand-supply imbalance and deflation pressure include: (1) liberalizing utility prices (along with fiscal subsidy); (2) consumption-supportive measures (e.g., product-specific fiscal support, fiscal subsidy for childcare and elderly care or incentives for giving birth, improving the social safety network, consumption vouchers or cash handouts); (3) income-growth measures; and (4) housing-stabilizing measures.

On monetary policy, our baseline assumes the next rate cut will come in March, although a February cut cannot be ruled out, especially considering the softer-than-expected January CPI reading. PBOC Governor Pan recently commented that one of the key objectives of monetary policy this year is to maintain general price stability and support a moderate price recovery.

## Credit report beat expectations

January<sup>’</sup>s credit report beat expectations, with a record-high TSF flow and new bank loan creation. TSF flow came in at an elevated 6.5tn yuan, with the growth rate of outstanding TSF unchanged, at 9.5%oya (Figure 2). Medium- and longterm corporate loans jumped 3.31tn yuan in January, likely reflecting the banking sector<sup>’</sup>s credit boost to complement recent macro policy support for infrastructure FAI, public housing and other targeted sectors. MLT loans for households also ticked up by 627bn yuan. For the non-loan TSF components, government bond issuance eased in January after the jump in 4Q, corporate bond issuance picked up, and shadow credit saw the strongest monthly increase since early 2017.

Figure 2: China TSF growth
![](images/221e9c29f2d29a9aca254ad8da75ca37332d08233bc0469c429d9d7f982b8e80.jpg)

Since late 4Q23, policymakers have implemented a series of support measures, including: (1) a 1tn yuan additional fiscal boost, funded by CGB issuance in 4Q; (2) PSL net injection of 500bn yuan (with 350bn yuan in December and 150bn yuan in January this year) to support public housing, urban village renovation and public infrastructure; and (3) structural

Tingting Ge (852) 2800-0143 tingting.ge@jpmorgan.com Ji Yan (852) 2800-7673 ji.yan@jpmorgan.com

(targeted) monetary instruments to support five key areas (technology; green economy; inclusive finance; elderly care; digital finance). The increase in MLT corporate loans in January likely reflects the banking sector<sup>’</sup>s credit boost to complement these macro policy supports. Meanwhile, the decent pickup in MLT household loans, amid lingering softness in new home sales, may partly reflect the recent decline in borrowers<sup>’</sup> early mortgage repayment trend, given banks<sup>’</sup> progress on rate-resetting for existing mortgages.

The central bank published the 4Q Monetary Policy Operation Report yesterday. On the credit policy front, the PBoC reiterated that “TSF and money supply growth should be in line with economic growth and inflation targets.<sup>”</sup> We expect 2024 TSF growth of 9.5%, modestly higher than the sum of the growth target (5%) and inflation target (3%) in our forecast, while the credit policy stance should be accommodative compared to our forecast of nominal GDP growth (5.3%) and inflation (0.6%).

Looking ahead, we expect two 10bp policy rate cuts, in 1Q and 3Q, respectively, along with another 25bp RRR cut in 4Q. Timing-wise, our baseline assumes the next rate cut will come in March, although a February cut cannot be ruled out, especially considering the softer-than-expected January CPI report. In addition, we believe structural monetary policy instruments will play an essential role in the PBOC<sup>’</sup>s policy operation this year. Overall, we maintain our forecast for stable TSF growth at 9.5% for 2024.

## FX reserves fell to \$3219bn

China<sup>’</sup>s January FX reserves came in largely in line with expectations, falling US\$18.7bn, to \$3219.3bn (Figure 3). This follows the \$136.8bn cumulative gain in November and December. The current account surplus is likely to have narrowed marginally. China Customs no longer releases monthly trade balance data for January and February separately, but instead releases combined data for the first two months in March. Incoming data of late suggest trade activities may have stayed solid from the year<sup>’</sup>s start amid stronger-than-expected external demand. The export orders component under both the NBS and Caixin manufacturing PMIs had 1.2-1.4pts gains in January. The high-frequency deadweight tonnage tracking of departing and arriving ships also points to solid trade momentum. We have penciled in a marginally narrower trade surplus and current account surplus (US\$24.1bn) for January.

Figure 3: China monthly change in FX reserves
![](images/a15aa998b97d5fbee533159e26f557f1833bcc0a7e2a1d8172a377982c8adef0.jpg)

The U.S. dollar index picked up from 101.3 to 103.3 in January, leading to an estimated currency valuation loss of US\$26.0bn. We estimate an implied capital outflow at US\$16.7bn, compared to the revised estimate of US\$5.9bn modest inflow for December. China has continued its gold purchases, with gold reserves rising for the 15th consecutive month through January. From November 2022 to January 2024, China increased its gold reserves by 9.55mn oz, or 15.2%. China<sup>’</sup>s UST holding amount ticked up by US\$12.4bn, to \$782.0bn in November 2023, but its share in China<sup>’</sup>s FX reserves slipped further, to 24.7%. Meanwhile, China<sup>’</sup>s share in total UST held by foreign investors also inched further lower, to 10.0%, compared to the peak of 28.2% in 2011.

## Taiwan: January CPI inflation eased

Headline CPI inflation eased to 1.79%oya in January (vs. 2.70%oya in December, Figure 4), or fell 0.1% m/m sa, partly reflecting the LNY seasonal factor. Food prices edged up 0.1% m/m sa (or up 4.1%oya). The aggregate energy-related CPI component fell 1.0% m/m sa (or up 0.5%oya). Core CPI inflation moderated to 1.64%oya in January, dragged on by certain categories of service prices with notable LNY-related volatility.

Figure 4: Taiwan headline and core CPI
![](images/855b5ca5da65c1b78d8c8ded89909b77b804c1a7adee0b3d5c245369828dd045.jpg)

We expect Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s headline and core CPI inflation rates to gradually ease going forward. Imported inflation will likely not be a key concern for Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s inflation outlook this year, and core CPI inflation will likely ease amid moderating

employment and domestic demand growth. CPI inflation will likely stay above 2% through 1H24 and moderate to below 2% toward 2H (especially by 4Q).

While the Fed will likely begin its rate cut cycle in June, we doubt the Taiwan central bank will rush to follow suit. We see a moderate possibility for the Taiwan central bank to consider a modest rate cut (of 12.5bp) going into 4Q, as CPI inflation will likely ease decisively below 2% (to average 1.6%oya in 4Q in our forecast).

## Exports modestly below expectations

Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s January merchandise exports came in modestly below expectations, rising 18.1%oya, following strong sequential growth in recent months (Figure 5). Seasonally adjusted, we estimate that exports (in volume terms) fell 2.5% m/m sa in January, following the significant gain of 5.7% m/ m sa in December. Overall tech exports fell 1.9% m/m sa in January, following a solid gain of 5.3% m/m sa in December. The breakdown highlights a strong underlying expansion trend in exports of information and communication products (+3.4% m/m sa in January, or up 105.4% 3m/3m saar), likely reflecting strong growth in exports of AI-related products. The breakdown by export destination highlights ongoing outperformance in exports to the U.S. (+3.1% m/m sa in January, or up 116.7% 3m/3m saar).

Figure 5: Taiwan nominal and real exports
![](images/cdfcee2ae2a3d36ae161cd2f72d91c9fc4f2bf1204a970a58fdff57fdd9ad933.jpg)

Imports showed significant growth in January, rising 16.6% m/m sa. While there may be some front-loading effect ahead of the LNY holiday period in February, the latest import strength broadly reflects: (1) a notable rise in capital goods imports, suggesting that the persistent capex weakness in recent quarters has likely bottomed and started to turn up; and (2) a solid gain in the imports of intermediate goods, including imports of tech-related intermediate inputs, hinting at solid export growth in the near term.

We look for further solid growth in Taiwan tech exports in the coming quarters, while the tech sector<sup>’</sup>s de-stocking pressure may begin to ease. The general upward trend in Taiwan<sup>’</sup>s merchandise exports in recent months somewhat contrasts

with cautious signals from recent export orders data. This likely reflects the latest boost of AI-related tech demand, which is mostly produced in Taiwan manufacturing plants, while the trend in overall export orders (including orders produced out of manufacturing plants in Mainland China) may be somewhat restrained by ongoing regional supply chain adjustments.

## Korea

• Current account continues gradual recovery...

• … with TOT tailwinds for goods trade surplus

• Locals returning to overseas investment

The balance of payments data for the final month of 2023 confirm a continuing recovery in the current account (CA) surplus in the latter part of last year. The CA surplus should remain on the rise, in our view, with a recovery in the terms of trade and normalization in net national savings. A tech cycle recovery implies that gains in export prices should modestly outpace those in import prices, with stabilizing global crude oil prices, suggesting tailwinds in the terms of trade. The gross investment rate is likely to fall after 2022/23<sup>’</sup>s high base due to the expected decrease in fiscal deficit-to-GDP ratio. Meanwhile, there are signs of locals regaining their appetite for foreign assets, which could lead to the recycling of higher CA inflows into financial outflows.

## TOT tailwinds for goods trade surplus

Korea<sup>’</sup>s current account (CA) recorded a US\$5.5bn surplus in December in seasonally adjusted terms, rebounding after a month of correction from October<sup>’</sup>s 1-1/2-year-high US\$6.8bn. Zooming out, the CA surplus has steadily risen to US\$5.0bn/month in 4Q23 from US\$1.5bn in 1H23 and US\$3.7bn in 3Q23 (Figure 1). In annual sums, it increased from a trough of US\$25.8bn in 2022 to US\$35.5bn in 2023. While it fell short of past years (US\$ 60bn-85bn in 2017- 2021), it came close to the lower end of the range if we focus only on the last quarter, which could be an encouraging sign that the country is gradually regaining its usual resilience in the current account surplus.

Figure 1: Korea current account balance
![](images/b581317e307d3a2216197405ecf20e2dda2faccafa4fa2ac73343074de136abc.jpg)

The overall CA recovered mainly through gains in merchandise trade in the past one year. The trade surplus has gradually widened since it ended a streak of deficits in April and marked a two-year high US\$6.8bn, sa, in December. It should continue to increase, in our view, with the ongoing tech upcycle (observed in both export value and volume) and stabilization in oil import prices (Figure 2). The latest customs data were also aligned with this view, as the seasonally adjusted customs trade surplus remained on the rise this January.

Figure 2: Korea trade balance and terms of trade
![](images/0f3b8ae23291cb42c5b00997842d1394fadc6bdc1fa39b9210f937b8df8d716c.jpg)
Source: BoK and J.P. Morgan

## Invisibles have returned to normal

Aside from goods, the services balance posted a deficit of US\$1.5bn, sa, in December, broadly stable within the range of US\$ 1.5bn-2.5bn/month in 2023. Its annual sum returned to the pre-pandemic level (US\$25.7bn deficit in 2023 vs. US\$26.8bn in 2019), shaped by a fall in freight transport receipts (with shipping rates settling back) and a rise in net travel imports. The travel deficit rose back to US\$12.5bn in 2023 (vs. US\$11.9bn in 2019) from US\$ 6bn-8bn in 2020- 2022. Meanwhile, primary income marked a modest US\$0.4bn, sa, surplus in December; it has adjusted downward after a tax reform led a one-off sharp increase in repatriated profits at the start of 2023 (Figure 3). That said, even the adjusted levels of US\$1.5bn/month in 2H23 were only marginally lower than the 2021-2022 average. The investment income surplus should maintain its multi-year rising trend in the medium term, with the accumulated foreign asset positions.

Figure 3: Korea current account breakdown
![](images/2cc89966f64f54a31b2fcbdf2bc0fc4c4f9e1f655f3500e3f3ad358c9f894e99.jpg)

## Locals returning to overseas investment

In the financial account, locals made outflows of US\$2.9bn for foreign stocks in December; the flows have gradually recovered to US\$3.1bn/month in 2H23 from a slump of US\$1.7bn/month in the prior one year (2H22-1H23). Outward FDI followed a similar pattern, as it recovered to US\$4.7bn in November and US\$5.8bn in December after hovering around an average of US\$2.4bn/month in the first ten months of 2023. This indicates that locals recently renewed their interest in overseas investment, likely in anticipation of interest rates peaking, after being discouraged earlier by rising rates.

Figure 4: Local residents' investment in foreign assets US\$ bn, +ve=outflows
![](images/cebb5c83630e7a03068d3eace1073f6a2cd2b3b088e058f8c693ae8b3499ed23.jpg)
Source: BoK, J.P. Morgan

Foreigners<sup>’</sup> investment in local stocks marked the second month of inflows, and its annual sum marked a US\$11.6bn surplus in 2023 after three years of outflows, likely on an improved outlook for a semiconductor industry cycle. Foreigners<sup>’</sup> bond inflows continued for eight months in the past nine, remaining stable at US\$26.3bn in 2023 compared to US\$24.9bn in 2022. Reserve assets increased by US\$1.4bn in December, but decreased by a cumulative US\$3.6bn in the full-year 2023.

## ASEAN

Malaysia’s domestic resilience continued through 4Q23 despite slowing in external demand

• Private consumption has been a key driver and presents a regional standout, with little signs of slowing

Diesel price adjustment could impact business costs but limited direct impact on consumer prices

• Net FX reserves stabilized at year-end, re-building FX buffers which suggests modest FX appreciation

The incoming data in Malaysia are generally positive, with domestic activity – particularly private consumption – remaining supportive despite lackluster external demand. This is evident across several indicators; motor vehicle sales, credit card billings and domestic production. We expect these trends to continue given the relatively heathy labor market trends, which we expect to slow over the course of this year.

However, the 2024 budget pencils in some energy subsidy rationalization and is reflected in a rise in headline inflation. Thus far, only the timeline for diesel price rationalization – which is utilized more by commercial than household end-users - has been announced, which is to be implemented during 2Q24. The timeframe for motor gasoline RON95 has yet to be announced and this is the fuel which has a larger representation in the consumer price basket and also for households. Thus, the outlook for consumer prices, real incomes and private consumption remains clouded by the prospective path of mogas subsidy rationalization.

In the absence of a substantial income boost, the supply side energy price adjustments are not expected to lead to a material rise in pricing power. This informs our view that core inflation should remain benign despite the rise in headline CPI and thus not elicit a monetary policy response. Aside from monetary policy, Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s net FX reserves rose in December to US\$75.3 billion, the highest level in 2023 and we expect that reserves accumulation rather than FX appreciation would be the policy bias given the likely desire to bolster its external resilience.

## Domestic resilience continues

In the past, we had characterized Malaysia as a small open economy and while this characterization still holds, it has exhibited unusual resilience this past year despite the slowing in external demand. Indeed, real exports contracted in sequential terms over the course of last year while overall growth remained broadly stable (Figure 1). Indeed, the high frequency data suggests that industrial production – which continues to be a significant driver of growth – did not exhibit the same softness as external demand (Figure 2). At a more granular level, this owes to the strength in production of domestically oriented products even as externally-oriented facing produc-

tion slowed (Figure 3).

![](images/194c62ff3352a2e410549ad71609ffca48c58dc35113a76fcae1153efe36eef0.jpg)

Figure 2: Malaysia industrial production and GDP %2Q, saar
![](images/840e08b70b7639db508ac5668bc159eeeeff9e0f71d62002783ab466b27373f3.jpg)

Figure 3: Malaysia industrial production by demand Index, 2019=100, sa
![](images/209a71851302257c7ddb887131de131483bd296aadca570470c3b61d77ecfa6c.jpg)

Figure 4: Malaysia real GDP Index, 2019=100, sa
![](images/a6f06b2afc9144fb49e04a5ac4c0ac74d6bfea7446558c23cfc2e69cb7ef2004.jpg)

In our view, this owes to the surprising resilience in domestic demand, especially private consumption, following its recovery in 2022 (Figure 4). Moreover, in relative terms, Malaysia<sup>’</sup>s consumption compares favorably to the rest of the

region, reflecting the relative outperformance of motor vehicle sales compared to the rest of ASEAN (Figure 5). That said, PCE growth of around 4-5% in the past year is slightly below its pre-COVID growth rate of 7%.

Figure 5: ASEAN motor vehicle sales
![](images/a02943b481d63caab4f98bd808760cdbbf8845e08f2eca56f45eded6149b1bce.jpg)

Figure 6: Malaysia employment and household savings
![](images/c1df1d3dd4c1fe45bb462809a95439c1d74018230fd4eff80fe585e88472aaca.jpg)

Figure 7: Malaysia credit card outstanding and overdue balances
![](images/f727c4c9cda6fec4f801ac1c973be696ff71c43f5354cd0abe523a7759a98de1.jpg)

The incoming employment and accrued savings data suggest that labor conditions are easing and savings are normalizing back to trend (Figure 6). Subsequently, we expect that PCE growth should remain at the lower bound of the 4-5% growth range and below the pre-COVID pace. However, the higher frequency credit card billings data remains on a solid uptrend which runs at odds with our expectations for slowing PCE. Moreover, despite the strong upturn, overdue balances remain close to historical lows suggesting that households are able to shoulder the payments without too much distress (Figure 7).

## Fuel price hikes coming, core in focus

An added headwind to consumption is the expectation that energy prices will be liberalized over the course of this year. The initial adjustment will likely come from diesel prices, which is predominantly consumed by commercial entities rather than households and has a smaller representation in the consumer basket. Given the current difference between domestic and market prices, the full price adjustment to market prices could be as much as 55-60% though a smaller adjustment is likely (Figure 8).

Figure 8: Malaysia and Singapore retail fuel prices Index, 4Q19=100, US\$ terms
![](images/536c469347d675772e0b02ba467ace41f19ffea8fb43875aa6bb67cba6157683.jpg)

Figure 9: Malaysia CPI
![](images/6f6dd070f7653db01d92b014a1a26a4779222387d44b48c78612b5ba998b8786.jpg)

Its impact is most likely to be felt in corporate/commercial margins than via a markup in final prices. This suggests some adjustment in energy prices, transportation prices but less for other goods given the limited pricing power amid easing nominal incomes. We pencil in a rise in headline CPI but not in core CPI and thus little need for the central bank to respond (Figure 9).

## FX reserves: rebuilding

Aside from our expectation that there will be little need for policy rate adjustment this year, the converse is likely the case for BNM<sup>’</sup>s FX reserves position, where the need to rebuild its FX reserves buffer will likely dominate (Figure 10). Such a reaction function will likely mean that FX appreciation will likely lag the rest of the region.

Figure 10: Malaysia FX reserves US\$ billion, both scales
![](images/8959ea01c81c0305ee92adff77a70468d597d352490370356319bc3bd30d728d.jpg)

## India

RBI keeps policy rate unchanged, sticks to “withdrawal of accommodation” stance

• Revises inflation lower with no softening in tone

• RBI<sup>’</sup>s liquidity operations push money market rates closer to policy rate

• Next week: CPI at 5.0% oya; IP at 3.3% oya

The February MPC meeting held policy rates at 6.5%, reiterated its “withdrawal of accommodation stance<sup>”</sup> and retained a cautious tone. We had expectations of a slightly softer tone. That said, in recent days, the RBI – through a series of finetuning liquidity operations – has helped push down overnight money-market rates from about 6.8% closer to the policy rate of 6.5%. This week<sup>’</sup>s meeting reinforces our view that, while liquidity operations will continue in the near term to ensure overnight money market rates stay closer to the policy rate, any policy rate easing will occur only later in the year. Next week we expect January CPI to ease to 5.0% from 5.7% on the back of softer food prices. Core prices are expected to be benign, projected to come in at 3.7% oya - lowest in 4 years. December IP is expected to come in at 3.3% oya, inching up from 2.4% in November.

## Liquidity helps ease overnight rates

We had expected that monetary policy tightening would be dialed-back in a three-step process, with the first step being ensuring that overnight money-market rates – which had consistently been trading 30-35 bps above the policy rate since September 2023 – were better aligned with the policy rate. That process has begun in earnest in recent days with the RBI actively undertaking short duration variable rate repos and reverse repo operations. Consequently, overnight rates have eased discernibly in recent days and are better aligned with the policy rate (see Figure 1).

Figure 1: Overnight money market rate
![](images/c58551f8311328f087dc3e42e9e278b11091c8607ccaa8bec6d14fc411aacfb3.jpg)

## RBI revises inflation forecasts lower

While we had not expected a stance change at this policy meeting, we believed the RBI<sup>’</sup>s tone, in general, might be softer with inflation tracking lower than it had envisaged (Figure 2). The RBI marked down its current quarter inflation from 5.2% to 5% and we expect the final outturn to be a shade even lower). Furthermore, the RBI projects 2024-25 inflation at 4.5% vis-a-vis 5.4% this fiscal year. In the event, though, the RBI hung tough on its tone, likely because its liquidity operations have already done some of the work by softening the effective policy rate.

Figure 2: RBI headline CPI inflation forecasts
![](images/e6a337be9de1f2995a9c4bd378d300d474855413dadc9eb341bac0bdf4959974.jpg)

We don<sup>’</sup>t expect the first cut till June, assuming the the Fed has moved by then. Furthermore, there is nothing that forces the RBI to change its stance to neutral before the first cut. It could always cut and change stance at the same meeting.

## January CPI expected at 5% oya

We expect January CPI to print at 5.0% oya, softening from 5.7% in December. Sequentially this would mean a 0.3% m/ m, sa increase after 0.6% last month. The softening in prices is underpinned by food which are projected to rise by 0.4% m/m, sa easing from 0.8% last month. The easing in food prices is led by vegetables. Core prices which have remained benign, averaging just 0.3% m/m, sa in the last nine months. We expect core prices to come in at 0.3% m/m, sa (0.2% in December), which would translate into 3.7% oya – the lowest print in four years. We expect December IP to print at 3.3% oya, inching up from 2.4% in November. This would mean a 0.7% m/m, sa increase. Recall that IP contracted in the last three consecutive months. Our expectation of improvement in IP is based on strong auto production data.

## Asia Focus: Manufacturing divergence

Since mid-2023, regional manufacturing and exports have posted a modest recovery (Figure 1), driven initially by the completion of the tech de-stocking cycle and more recently, optimism of a sustained global expansion.

Figure 1: EM Asia manufacturing PMI vs. IP and exports
![](images/080640e4835927156d04db564b75ebcc7919af820bfbf493b11ed04eba2df1f9.jpg)

However, the regional manufacturing recovery has lacked breadth so far, concentrating mostly in tech (Figure 2). Even within sectors, the dispersion in performance between countries is high, for example, between Korea/Taiwan and Japan/ Thailand in tech (Figure 3) and between Japan and Thailand in autos (Figure 4).

Secular forces, ranging from rising electric vehicle (EV) market penetration to the on-shoring of high-end semiconductor production in Taiwan, likely play a significant role in driving these dispersions. Differences in cyclical position are also a factor. In the case of Thailand, despite stable external demand, factory output has contracted (Figure 5) due to elevated finished goods inventories (Figure 6).

Figure 2: Asia¹ manufacturing output
![](images/be6366692e81973b7c17d8697b440a767674ca635f1b05a7a5f93d2ebaedfde2.jpg)

Figure 3: Asia tech production Index, 2015=100, sa
![](images/51baa8ffb7184474810776c24025e2ad52ee7d60880ef11c645de3d9ee5115d5.jpg)

Figure 4: Asia auto manufacturing output Index, 2015=100, 2mma, sa
![](images/ddb159a6dc538f1622444e5e44fef62800cbad51503528b09d0aa348566aa45c.jpg)

Figure 5: Thailand manufacturing production vs. real exports Dec19=100, sa Real ex
![](images/3816052a5336ddb5d85109358c22104bf20eac2800181f5934d2b7f73cb59e91.jpg)

Figure 6: Thailand manufacturing sector
![](images/ee03ed159b6275e25f733b82407939203ebb0105067d7847606eeb27062b2fcc.jpg)
